3.3 Monte-Carlo sample simulation
This section highlights the simulated event samples for signal higgsino production pro￾cesses and background processes used in the analysis. The simulation is done using
Monte-Carlo (MC) methods; the simulated event samples are hereinafter referred to as
MC samples in this dissertation.
3.3.1 Signal MC sample generation
As mentioned in Section 1.3.1, the search presented in this dissertation assumes a simpli￾fied higgsino-like LSP model, a typical case where the LSP is dominated by the higgsino
component. In such a model, the masses of the higgsino triplet (˜￾0
2,˜￾±
1 ,˜￾0
1) have the
following relations:
• m(˜￾0
2) >m(˜￾±
1 ) >m(˜￾0
1),
• m(˜￾±
1 )= 1
2
￾
m(˜￾0
2)+ m(˜￾0
1)
 
.
Thus, the signal MC samples have been generated with the above assumptions. All other
SUSY particles are assumed to be much heavier and not have any phenomenological rela￾tions with the higgsinos. Total of 28 mass points have been considered to target the unex￾plored regions: the considered mass splitting values are ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1)=1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 3.0, 4.0,
and 5.0 GeV, and the considered masses are m(˜￾0
1) = 85, 100, 125, 150, and 175 GeV.
Fig. 3.2 shows an overall view of the mass points used for signal MC sample generation,
compared to the exclusion limits set by other searches. For each of the mass points, sep￾arate MC samples have been generated to properly calculate the cross sections for each
of the three production processes: ˜￾0
2 ￾˜0
1,˜￾0
2 ￾˜+
1 , and ˜￾0
2 ￾˜￾
1 .
The signal MC samples have been generated at leading order (LO) with MG5 aMC@NLO
2.9.3 [124] using the NNPDF23LO [125] parton distribution function (PDF) set. The spin
of the higgsinos were conserved using MadSpin [126]. The events were then interfaced
with Pythia 8.245 [127] to model parton showering, hadronization, and underlying events
using A14 tune [128]. The matching between the matrix element and parton shower was
performed using the CKKW-L scheme [129], with the merging scale set to 15 GeV.
For e￾cient generation of the targeted event topology, several requirements have been
imposed during the generation step. First, due to the ISR topology targeted by this search,
the samples are generated only for events associated with at least one hadronic jet with
pT > 50 GeV. The e↵ect of this requirement on the production cross section is considered
by applying a dedicated correction factor, later described in this section. Moreover, as an
event filter, the transverse component of the momentum sum of non-interacting particles
(corresponding to Emiss
T at reconstruction level) is required to be more than 50 GeV.
One lepton (electron or muon) filter with pT > 2 GeV are also applied to ensure high
production e￾ciency of the targeted high Emiss
T and one lepton topology. For these two
filters, corresponding filter e￾ciencies are applied to the samples, ranging from 10% to
70%, with high dependence on the mass splitting value.
The production cross sections and their uncertainties are calculated to NLO+NLL
accuracy using Resummino [130–135]. Since these cross sections do not account for the
ISR jet as mentioned above, they must be corrected by a factor of ￾(￾ 1 jet)/￾(￾ 0 jet).
51

=== PDF PAGE 63 ===
Figure 3.2: Overall view of the signal mass points used in the search in the m(˜￾±
1 ) vs ￾m(˜￾±
1 , ￾˜0
1) plane.
The mass points have been chosen to search for regions with small mass splitting values, not yet explored
by other searches.
This correction factor is derived for each signal point by preparing low statistics samples
which include the 0-jet process, and their typical values range from 0.2 to 0.3. The
corrected channel inclusive cross sections range from about 300 fb for the lowest mass point
to about 30 fb for the highest mass point, satisfying the relation ￾(˜￾0
2￾˜+
1 ) > ￾(˜￾0
2￾˜0
1) >
￾(˜￾0
2￾˜￾
1 ).
3.3.2 Background MC sample generation
As later described in Section 7, although the dominant background contribution from
“fake” lepton tracks, mainly misidentified hadronic tracks, is estimated using a data￾driven method, small contribution of “true” lepton tracks, actual leptons coming from
physics processes, is estimated using background MC samples. Table 3.3 shows the list
of background MC samples used in the analysis. Note that processes with little “true”
contribution, such as tt
¯, single top, dijet, and ￾+jets, have also been included just in case,
but were later found to be negligible.
Sherpa [136] and NNPDF30NNLO [125] PDF sets are used for the modeling of W,
Z, and ￾ production associated with hadronic jets, as well as diboson (WZ, ZZ,WW)
production processes. W+jets and Z+jets processes are generated with Sherpa 2.2.11,
whereas ￾+jets processes are generated with Sherpa 2.2.2. Diboson processes are mainly
generated with Sherpa 2.2.2 but partially generated with Sherpa 2.2.1.
Powheg [137–139] interfaced with Pythia 8.2 [127] for showering is used for single
top production (s-channel, t-channel, and tW), tt
¯, and dijet processes. For these processes,

3.3.3 Detector and pileup simulation
All signal and background MC samples are processed through the full ATLAS simula￾tion framework [140], which uses Geant4 [141]. Simulation of the e↵ects of pileup is
performed by generating soft QCD processes separately and overlaying them to each of
the hard-scatter events. The MC samples are then reweighted to have a matching pileup
distribution with observed data, with respect to the number of pileup through the data￾taking periods.
53

=== PDF PAGE 65 ===
4. Standard Object Reconstruction and
Identification
For both recorded data and simulated MC samples, detector-level information is processed
and translated into physics-level “objects” through reconstruction and identification. This
section summarizes the reconstruction and identification of standard objects used in this
search.
4.1 Track
The trajectories of charged particles are reconstructed as tracks using the hit information
in the Inner Detectors. The tracks used in this search are standard tracks reconstructed
by the primary “inside out” algorithm [142]. For each layer of Pixel and SCT detectors,
the algorithm first combines signals into clusters, which are further converted to three
dimensional “space points”. Next, track seeds are formed from three space points, ex￾pected to be originating from the same charged track trajectory. Track seeds are then
extended through a combinatorial Kalman filter [143], which yield a set of potential track
candidates. Since these candidates still have overlaps, an ambiguity resolution algorithm
and a subsequent global ￾2 fit method are applied to obtain the final tracks.
After the final track candidates are obtained from the Pixel and SCT hits, extensions
to the TRT detector is attempted. TRT extension is performed in a similar way using
a Kalman filter and a global ￾2 fit. At least nine TRT hits are required to perform the
extension, and the extension may be rejected when the fit quality is detrimentally a↵ected.
Tracks are described by five parameters using a perigee representation as shown in
Fig. 4.1. d0 and z0 are the transverse and longitudinal impact parameters with respect
to the primary vertex, described later in Section 4.2. ￾ and ✓ are the azimuth and polar
angles similar to those defined in Section 2.2.1. q/p is the charge of the track divided
by the magnitude of its momentum, measured from the curvature of the track under the
solenoid magnetic field.
Preselection requirements in this search
The following preselection requirements, defined as the TightPrimary working point, are
imposed to the tracks used in the analysis.
• pT > 500 MeV
• |⌘| < 2.5
54

=== PDF PAGE 66 ===
Figure 4.1: Schematic diagram of a track in the ATLAS detector and its parameters.
55

=== PDF PAGE 67 ===
• NSi ￾ 7: At least 7 hits in the silicon detectors (Pixel and SCT, including dead
sensors)
• Nsh
mod  1: Less than or equal to 1 Pixel/SCT hits assigned to multiple tracks
• Nhole
Si  2: Less than or equal to 2 Pixel/SCT hits missing
• NSi ￾ 9 (if |⌘| < 1.65): At least 9 hits in the silicon detectors (if |⌘| < 1.65)
• NSi ￾ 11 (if |⌘| > 1.65): At least 11 hits in the silicon detectors (if |⌘| > 1.65)
• NIBL+NB-Layer > 0: At least 1 hit in the two innermost layers (IBL or B-Layer) if
expected
• Nhole
pix = 0: No Pixel hits missing
Further track selection criteria used in the analysis are discussed later in Section 6.
4.2 Vertex
The positions where the proton-proton collisions occurred, often referred to as the primary
vertices, must be reconstructed for each event to find the “hard-scatter” vertex and provide
a reference point for object reconstruction. Primary vertex reconstruction is performed
using vertex finding and fitting algorithms, exploiting reconstructed track information
[144].
A vertex seed is first found from the maximum in the z coordinate distribution of the
reconstructed tracks. Then, the vertex seed and its surrounding tracks are used in the
adaptive vertex fitting algorithm [145] for determining the vertex position. Tracks incom￾patible with the found vertex is used to seed another vertex, and the finding procedure
is repeated until all tracks are associated to vertices, each required to have at least two
associated tracks. Out of the multiple primary vertices found by these procedures, one
vertex with the highest sum of associated track pT is considered as the “hard-scatter”
vertex and is used as the reference point of object calibrations.
4.3 Topo-cluster
Particle energy measurement in the calorimeters are performed for each three-dimensional
“topo-cluster”, which is a cluster of topologically connected calorimeter cell signals [146].
The formation of topo-clusters is performed by a three dimensional growing-volume al￾gorithm starting from a seed cell with high energy deposit. Neighboring cells of the seed
cell are added to the cluster if the cell signal has a energy deposit exceeding two times
the expected noise signal. The neighboring cells are then considered as the seed cells
for the next iteration of the growth. The growth continues until the all cells exceeding
the threshold are collected. If two growing clusters overlap, they are merged into one
cluster. The cluster formed by the this algorithm could be too large to provide precise
measurement of the particle energy. Hence, cluster splitting is applied if there are two or
more local signal maxima defined by Ecell > 500 MeV.
56

=== PDF PAGE 68 ===
4.4 Jet
Reconstruction and calibration
Gluons and quarks produced by the proton-proton interactions are reconstructed as groups
of hadrons called “jets” as a result of hadronization. Jets are reconstructed using charged
tracks and topo-clusters with dedicated algorithms. First using the particle flow algorithm
[147], tracks and topo-clusters are matched accordingly, and cell-based energy subtraction
is performed to remove overlaps between the momentum and energy measurements in
the Inner Detector and calorimeters. Next, using the tracks originating from the hard￾scatter vertex and topo-clusters surviving the energy subtraction in particle flow, jets are
reconstructed using the anti-kt algorithm [148] with radius parameter R =0.4.
Since the energy of the jets are reconstructed at electromagnetic scale, jet energy scale
calibration [149] is performed to correct the measured jet energy. Fig. 4.2 shows the
stages of jet energy scale calibrations. Pileup corrections are first conducted to subtract
contributions of pileup from the jet pT. This is followed by the absolute MC-based cali￾bration, which corrects the reconstructed jet four-momentum to the particle-level energy
scale, considering detector response e↵ects. Global sequential calibration is later per￾formed to reduce dependencies on the initiating and constituent particles. Finally, in situ
calibrations are applied to data to correct for the di↵erences between data and MC.
Figure 4.2: Stages of jet energy scale calibrations [149].
Jet vertex tagging
Pileup mitigation is necessary to suppress jets which do not originate from the hard￾scatter event. For the jets used in the analysis, jet vertex tagging (JVT) [150] is applied
for this purpose. JVT is constructed using a two-dimensional likelihood in the corrected
jet vertex fraction (corrJVF) and RpT plane. The corrJVF variable is defined as the
scalar pT sum of the tracks associated with the jet and originate from the hard-scatter
vertex (PV0), divided by the scalar pT sum of the associated tracks that originate from
all interactions including pileup [150]:
corrJVF =

Flavor tagging
Jet flavor tagging plays a significant role in identifying physics processes. In particular,
separation of jets containing b-hadrons and c-hadrons (b- and c-jets) from light-flavor jets
(u-, d-, and s-jets) and gluon jets is of high importance. In this search, flavor tagging is
used to veto events with b-jets to suppress tt
¯ backgrounds.
Flavor tagging algorithms exploit the long lifetime of b- and c-hadrons, generally re￾sulting in a displaced secondary vertex, few mm away from the hard-scatter primary
vertex. First, low-level algorithms reconstruct the characteristics of b-and c-jets using
properties of tracks and displaced vertices. IP2D and IP3D [151] are low-level algorithms
which use the impact parameters of individual tracks associated with the jets. RNNIP
algorithm [152] uses recurrent neural networks (RNNs) to further consider the correla￾tion of impact parameters between tracks; if one track has a large impact parameter, it
is likely that other associated tracks also have large impact parameters. SV1 [153] is a
secondary vertex tagging algorithm that reconstructs a displaced secondary vertex within
a jet, using a ￾2 test. JetFitter [154] uses a modified Kalman filter [143] to reconstruct
the full b- and c-hadron decay chain by exploiting the topological features within the jet.
58

=== PDF PAGE 70 ===
The subsequent high-level algorithm combines the outputs of the low-level algorithms
using multivariate classifiers. This search uses the DL1r algorithm [155], which combines
the results of IP2D, IP3D, RNNIP, SV1, and JetFitter, using a fully connected NN. The
DL1r NN outputs the probabilities for a jet to be a b-jet, a c-jet, or a light-flavor jet,
defined as pb, pc, and plight, respectively.
This search uses b-jet tagging to veto events with b-jets, mainly to suppress tt
¯processes.
The final discriminant of b-tagging is defined as
D = ln ✓ pb
fc · pc + (1 ￾ fc) · plight◆
, (4.3)
where fc is the e↵ective c-jet fraction, optimized to 0.018. Fig. 4.4 shows the b-jet tagging
e￾ciency for various working points. This search uses the 85% working point, which the
final discriminant D is fixed to a value for which 85% of the b-jets in a tt
¯ sample have a
higher score.
Figure 4.4: B-jet tagging e￾ciency for various working points as a function of b-jet pT [155].
Jet cleaning
In searches using leading jets, imposing certain criteria on the quality of the jets help
suppress non-collision background processes [156]. The implementation of these criteria
are known as jet cleaning.
Good and fake jets can be discriminated using track and calorimeter based variables.
In particular, the following variables are used. The jet charged fraction fch is defined
as the scalar sum of the pT of tracks, originating from the primary vertex associated
to the jet, divided by the pT of that jet. In addition, the energy fraction in the layer
with maximum energy deposit in the calorimeters, denoted as fmax, is defined. The ratio
between these variables, fch/fmax, is required to be small for good quality jets. In the
Tight working point used in this search and later mentioned in Section 6.2, fch/fmax < 0.1
is required for jets with |⌘| < 2.4.
59

=== PDF PAGE 71 ===
Preselection requirements in this search
The following preselection requirements are imposed to the jets used in the analysis.
• All jets are required to have pT > 20 GeV and |⌘| < 4.5.
• For jets having pT < 60 GeV and |⌘| < 2.4, JVT > 0.5 is required for pileup
mitigation.
• B-jets are required to have pT > 20 GeV and |⌘| < 2.5 and are identified using the
DL1r 85% working point,
Further selection criteria used in the analysis are discussed later in Section 6.
4.5 Electron
When electrons pass through material, they radiate photons through Bremsstrahlung, and
the generated photons create electrons through pair production, resulting in an electro￾magnetic (EM) shower. This mainly occurs in the EM calorimeter in the ATLAS detector,
but could also occur more upstream, inside the Inner Detector. Thus, the reconstruction
of electrons is performed using track and EM cluster information. Fig. 4.5 shows the path
of an electron inside the ATLAS detector.
Figure 4.5: Schematic illustration of the electron path within the ATLAS detector [157].
Electron reconstruction starts o↵ with seed-cluster reconstruction in the EM calorime￾ter. This is done using a sliding-window algorithm [158], which seeds energy cluster
candidates from localized energy deposits by scanning over the whole EM calorimeter.
The seed-cluster candidates are then matched to modified tracks, which account for the
energy loss in material through an optimzed Gaussian-sum filter (GSF) [159]. Finally,
reconstructed clusters are formed around the seed clusters, and energy calibration is per￾formed using multivariate algorithms [160].
Identification of electrons are performed using a likelihood-based (LH) method [157],
which uses information from tracking, calorimetry, and combinations of both as inputs.
The final discriminant for identification is formed by transforming the signal likelihood
60

=== PDF PAGE 72 ===
fraction using an inverse sigmoid function. By setting fixed thresholds on this discrim￾inant, three working points are defined for electrons with ET > 4.5 GeV, as shown in
Fig. 4.6: Loose, Medium, and Tight. The e￾ciencies for identifying a prompt electron
with ET = 40 GeV are 93%, 88%, and 80% for each of the working points, respectively.
In addition, a variation of the Loose working point is defined by requiring a hit in the
innermost layer of the Pixel detector and is called LooseAndBLayer.
(a) E￾ciency vs ET (b) E￾ciency vs ⌘
Figure 4.6: Electron identification e￾ciencies for various working points as a function of ET and ⌘ [161].
Preselection requirements in this search
The following preselection requirements are imposed to the electrons used in this analysis.
• All electrons are required to have pT > 4.5 GeV and |⌘| < 2.47.
• All electrons are required to pass the LooseAndBLayer identification working point.
• All electrons must have |z0 sin ✓| < 0.5 mm, likely originating from the hard-scatter
vertex.
Further selection criteria used in the analysis are discussed later in Section 6.
4.6 Muon
Muon reconstruction is mainly based on information from the tracks in the Inner Detector
and the measurements in the muon spectrometers [162, 163]. The matching between
tracks in the Inner Detector and the tracks in the muon spectrometers is performed by a
combined track fit based on the hits in both Inner Detector and muon spectrometers, also
considering the energy loss in the calorimeters. This is done using an outside-in approach,
61

=== PDF PAGE 73 ===
in which a muon reconstructed in the muon spectrometers is extrapolated inwards and
matched to a track in the Inner Detector.
Muons are identified by imposing various requirements on features such as the number
of hits in the Inner Detector and muon spectrometers, track fit properties, and variables
defined to test the compatibilities between individual measurements in the Inner Detector
and muon spectrometers. For instance, the q/p compatibility [163], defined as
q/p compatibility = |q/pID ￾ q/pMS|
p￾2(q/pID)+ ￾2(q/pMS)
, (4.4)
is required to be small for muons, where q/pID(MS) and ￾(q/pID(MS)) represent the q/p
and its uncertainty measured in the Inner Detector (muon spectrometers), respectively.
Five identification working points are defined for muons with pT > 3 GeV and |⌘| < 2.7:
Loose, Medium, Tight, High-pT, and Low-pT [163]. Fig. 4.7 shows the muon identification
e￾ciencies for Medium and Low-pT working points, specifically for low-pT muons.
(a) E￾ciency vs pT (b) E￾ciency vs ⌘

• All muons are required to pass the Medium identification working point to realize

• All muons must have |z0 sin ✓| < 0.5 mm, likely originating from the hard-scatter

4.7 Overlap removal
Since the reconstruction of multiple physics objects are performed individually, this could
potentially result in ambiguities and overlaps between objects. To avoid the “double￾counting” of each object, these ambiguities are resolved by performing sequential proce￾dures known as “overlap removal (OR)”. The removal procedures are carried out in the fol￾lowing order, using the angular distance between objects defined as dR = p(￾⌘)2 +(￾￾)2.
Note that overlap removals for photons and tau-leptons are not applied, because they are
not explicitly used in the event selection described in Section 6.
Muon-electron OR
Reject the muon if its Inner Detector track is shared with an electron.
Jet-electron OR
Reject the jet if not a b-jet and dR < 0.2.
Electron-jet OR
Reject the electron if dR < min(0.4, 0.04 + 10 GeV/pT(e)).
Jet-muon OR
Reject the jet if not a b-jet and dR < 0.2.
Muon-jet OR
Reject the muon if dR < min(0.4, 0.04 + 10 GeV/pT(µ)).
4.8 Missing transverse momentum
Particles that do not interact with strong or electromagnetic force will likely pass through
the ATLAS detector without leaving any trace. These include SM particles such as neu￾trinos and BSM particles such as neutralinos. However, since the transverse momentum
of the initial p-p collision system at the LHC is zero, they can be indirectly detected as
missing transverse momentum pmiss
T with its magnitude Emiss
T .
Missing transverse momentum is reconstructed as the negative vector sum of all visible
objects, which consist of hard and soft objects. The hard objects are comprised of elec￾trons (e), photons (￾), hadronically decaying tau leptons (⌧had), muons (µ), and jets (j).
The term considering soft objects, often referred to as the soft term, consider additional
contributions from charged tracks which are from the hard-scatter vertex and are unused
in the reconstruction of other objects. Thus, the missing transverse momentum can be
written as [164]
pmiss
T = ￾
Xpe
T ￾Xp￾
T ￾Xp⌧had
T ￾Xpµ
T ￾Xpj
T ￾ psoft term
T , (4.5)
63

=== PDF PAGE 75 ===
in which the sum over each object is taken over those passing certain criteria, specifically
described in Ref. [164]. In addition, the missing transverse momentum used in the analysis
is defined as the Tight working point, in which forward jets are required to satisfy pT >
30 GeV.

5. Development of Low-pT Lepton Track
Identification
One of the main e↵orts of the search presented in this dissertation is the development
of the low-pT electron and muon track identification methods, which aim to identify the
low-pT electrons and muons emitted from the signal ˜￾0
2 ! ￾˜0
1`+`￾ decay. They serve
significant roles in this search; as the emitted electrons and muons have very low-pT as
shown in Fig. 5.1, the sensitivity to higgsinos with target mass splitting values largely
depends on the performance of these identification algorithms.
(a) Electron (b) Muon

5.1 Low-pT electron track
Standard electron identification algorithm described in Section 4.5 has a lower pT thresh￾old of 4.5 GeV to suppress hadronic noise in the calorimeters. However, target higgsino
signals having a typical mass splitting ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1) of a few GeV tend to emit electrons
(and muons, which is discussed later in Section 5.2) with lower pT. Therefore, a new
low-pT electron track identification algorithm, dedicated to this search, has been newly
developed. The new algorithm targets electron tracks having pT =0.5–5 GeV. This
section describes the details of the low-pT electron identification algorithm, its selection
criteria, and its e￾ciency calibration.
65

=== PDF PAGE 77 ===
5.1.1 Identification algorithm
The new low-pT electron track identification (hereinafter referred to as LPET ID) targets
tracks having pT down to 0.5 GeV, which is the lower bound required for charged tracks.
Considering that some of the electrons, having pT larger but close to the 4.5 GeV threshold,
escape the standard electron ID, the new LPET ID targets tracks having pT =0.5–5 GeV.
Tracks in the target pT range is dominated by hadrons (mostly charged pions) and fake
tracks. The idea of LPET ID is to distinguish electron-like tracks from these background
tracks by exploiting their characteristics. The characteristics of tracks can be roughly
categorized into two groups: silicon (Pixel and SCT) detector response and TRT detector
response. Since the detector response of TRT has a large impact on electron identification
as described in Section 2.2.3, its information is actively used for tracks with successful
extension. In addition to this tracking information, energy deposits in the electromagnetic
calorimeter are used by matching each track to the corresponding topo-cluster. LPET
ID has been designed to utilize these track and calorimeter variables as inputs to classify
electron-like tracks.
Track to calorimeter cluster position extrapolation
Although target tracks have very low pT, the bulk is expected to reach the calorimeter in
principle. It is therefore plausible to also use the calorimeter information as inputs of the
LPET ID by extrapolating each track to its corresponding topo-cluster. The standard
extrapolation method of tracks to the calorimeter has been briefly extended to support
the target pT range using a dedicated position matching method.
The basic idea of this method is to simply match the track to its corresponding cluster
based on its relative position coordinates ￾⌘ ⌘ ⌘calo ￾ ⌘track and ￾￾ ⌘ ￾calo ￾ ￾track;a
schematic diagram of the latter is shown in Fig. 5.2. Here, (⌘track, ￾track) and (⌘calo, ￾calo)
correspond to the orientation of the particle at the interaction point and at the calorimeter,
respectively. Considering the barrel and endcap topology of the calorimeters and also the
curvature of electrons in 2 T magnetic field, candidate clusters for each track are required
to pass the following loose position requirements:
• |⌘calo ￾ ⌘track| < 0.1,
• cos(￾calo ￾ ￾track) > 0.8,
• |sin(￾￾)ext| < 0.2, where
sin(￾￾)ext ⌘ sin(￾calo ￾ ￾track)+0.45 · trackCharge
trackPt [GeV] (if |⌘track| < 1.5),
sin(￾￾)ext ⌘ sin(￾calo ￾ ￾track)+ 2
e1.13|⌘|￾e￾1.13|⌘| · trackCharge
trackPt [GeV] (if |⌘track| > 1.5).
The first requirement on ￾⌘ simply requires the track and the calo cluster to be close to
each other, whereas the second and third requirements on ￾￾ consider the electron track
curvature in the plane perpendicular to the beampipe.
Cluster selection using energy deposits
In addition to the position requirements, cuts on the energy deposits of calorimeter lay￾ers are imposed to preselect electron-like topo-clusters, which tend to have more energy

the plane perpendicular to the beam pipe.

than the energy deposit Ei itself. Table 5.1 shows the list of calorimeter layers and their
preselection requirements. Fig. 5.3 shows the energy ratio distributions of electron and

. Two-dimensional distributions of ￾￾ext vs ￾⌘ are

only used for the training and not used for the search; the preselected cluster having the
highest LPET ID score is chosen for the search.
Figure 5.4: Simulated number of topo-clusters passing the position extrapolation and energy deposit

electron tracks do not have any associated topo-clusters.
Input features
The list of the input variables used for the LPET ID are shown on Table 5.2. The inputs
are roughly categorized into three groups: track, TRT, and calo inputs. Track inputs are
available for every track, whereas the TRT and calo inputs are available only when the
extension to the TRT and to the calorimeter is successfully performed, respectively.
The distributions of these variables have been checked to select which variables to use
as inputs to the LPET ID. It has been seen that the fitting error variables of track inputs,
many of the TRT inputs, and the energy deposits in the inner calorimeter layers have high
importances for discriminating low-pT electron tracks against low-pT background tracks.
Fig. 5.6 shows the distributions of fitting error variables of the track inputs. Due
to e↵ects of Bremsstrahlung, the fitting errors of the track position tend to be larger
for electron tracks compared to background hadron tracks. It has also been seen that
this e↵ect is more apparent if tracks with the same “actual” (or truth) pT are compared;
electron tracks tend to have smaller reconstructed pT due to Bremsstrahlung, resulting in
larger values of fitting errors.

Fig. 5.7 shows the distributions of TRT inputs with high importance. Many of the
TRT inputs have strong separation power, since the TRT is capable of detecting transition
radiation of the electrons, as described in Section 2.2.3. TRT hits with high transition
radiation are detected as “high threshold hits”, and the number of such hits is larger
for electrons. The probability of the track being an electron is also calculated from the
high threshold hits as “eProbabilityHT”. Other TRT inputs such as dE/dx also have
separation power.

Fig. 5.8 shows the distributions of energy deposit ratios in the inner layers of the EM

to background tracks, because electrons are more likely to induce electromagnetic showers
and deposit more energy.

=== PDF PAGE 83 ===
Classification with deep neural network
A deep neural network (DNN) algorithm has been developed to maximize the identifica￾tion e￾ciency of electron-like tracks by exploiting the track and its associated topo-cluster
variables. Fig. 5.9 shows the overall structure of the LPET ID DNN. The algorithm bi￾narily classifies whether the input track is electron-like or non-electron-like; an output
electron likeliness score ranging from 0 to 1 is calculated based on the 79 input variables.
The TRT variables and calorimeter variables may not be available for some tracks due to
detector coverage and noise thresholds; in this case, these variables are set to 0. All of
the input values are standardized according to the transformation
z = x ￾ µ
￾ , (5.1)
where x is the raw input value, µ is the average of that input in the training dataset, ￾
is the standard deviation of that input in the training dataset, and z is the standardized
input used for the DNN.
Figure 5.9: Overall structure of LPET ID DNN. 79 variables are used as inputs, and there are two hidden
layers with 128 nodes. The nodes are fully connected. The output electron likeliness score ranges from 0
to 1, with 1 meaning that the track is fully electron-like.
The training of LPET ID DNN has been done using MC “SingleParticleGun” electron
events, where there is only one electron per event at the stage of event generation. To
consider the e↵ects of pileup collisions on track reconstruction in the training, the events
were reconstructed by overlaying pileup collisions as described in Section 3.3.3. The signal
electron tracks used for the training were sampled from these events by dR matching
between the tracks and the truth electron particle. The background tracks used for the
training were also sampled from these events by vetoing the dR matched signal tracks.
For each signal and background tracks, about 4.5M samples were used for the training,
where 10% were assigned as validation samples. The training configurations of the neural
network; which have been optimized together with the model structure, are summarized
in Table 5.3. The accuracy and loss values [165] during the training procedure are shown
in Fig. 5.10; no overtraining has been observed.

5.1.2 Optimization of selection criteria
Because the LPET ID was trained using SingleParticleGun samples, it is necessary to
adapt the LPET ID to the kinematics of the target signal. Therefore, optimized selection
criteria targeting electron tracks from ˜￾0
2 ! ￾˜0
1Z⇤ decay needs to be defined. Since the
LPET ID DNN score may have some dependency on the track pT and ⌘, it is ideal to define
criteria depending on these three variables. This section describes the criteria definition
and the corresponding performance.
LPET ID WP DNN
To dynamically define selection criteria or “working point (WP)” dependent on the LPET
ID DNN score, track pT, and track ⌘, another DNN algorithm (hereinafter referred to as
LPET ID WP DNN) has been developed. Fig. 5.12 shows the overall structure of LPET
ID WP DNN. The WP DNN is designed to binarily classify whether the input track is
an electron coming from ˜￾0
2 ! ￾˜0
1Z⇤ decay or not; an output score is calculated based on
the three inputs. The inputs are standardized following Eq. (5.1).
75

=== PDF PAGE 87 ===
Figure 5.12: Overall structure of LPET ID WP DNN. Three variables are used as inputs: track pT, track
⌘ and LPET ID DNN score. The structure is similar to that of LPET ID DNN, having fully connected
nodes and two hidden layers with 128 nodes. The output WP score ranges from 0 to 1, with 1 meaning
that the track is fully likely to be an electron from ˜￾0
2 ! ￾˜0
1Z⇤ decay.
The training of LPET ID WP DNN has been done using MC events passing the
1L1T selection criteria mentioned later in Section 6. The signal electron tracks were
sampled from higgsino production events by requiring dR matching between the tracks
and the truth electron particle originating from ˜￾0
2. All mass points and production
channels mentioned in Section 3.3.1 have been used to ensure enough statistics and to
achieve training compatible to all masses. The background tracks were sampled from
W+jets events, which is the main SM background process of this search. All signal and
background tracks are required to pass the track selection criteria also mentioned later in
Section 6. For each signal and background tracks, 42k samples were used for the training,
where 20% were assigned as validation samples. The training configurations of the WP
DNN are summarized in Table 5.4. The distributions of the input variables are shown in
Fig. 5.13.
Table 5.4: Summary of the training configurations of LPET ID WP DNN.

e￾ciency is 30–40%, while the background e￾ciency is about 0.5%.

5.1.3 Performance measurement with data and e￾ciency cali￾bration
To apply the LPET ID WP in the analysis, its e￾ciency must be calibrated with data.
This calibration has been done using Z ! ee￾ events. The section briefly summarizes
the calibration method and its results.
Target event topology
For the calibration of LPET ID, a well-known SM process having a low-pT electron track
must be used as a control sample. Z ! ee￾ events are introduced for this purpose,
considering its event topology with low background level. Fig. 5.16 shows a diagram
representing Z ! ee￾ events. In this evaluation, only events with the following topology
is targeted; the electron closer to the photon is required to have very low pT, and the
other electron and the photon take away most of the energy.
Figure 5.16: Diagram representing Z ! ee￾ events used for the calibration of LPET ID. The electron
closer to the photon is required to have very low pT for target events.
Event and track selection criteria
Events are selected using single electron trigger with trigger matching applied. Emiss
T <
50 GeV and b-tagged jet veto are required to suppress background events. A single
electron and a single photon are required for each event; the electron must pass the Tight
WP and have 30 <pT < 60 GeV, and the photon must pass the Tight WP and have
30 <pT < 55 GeV. Additional cuts on kinematic variables such as the electron-photon
invariant mass m(e, ￾) and dR(e, ￾) are imposed to select Z ! ee￾ -like events.
Target tracks are required to pass the TightPrimary WP (defined in Section 4.1) and
have charges opposite to that of the electron. Usual pT and ⌘ requirements are applied:
0.5 <pT < 5 GeV and |⌘| < 2.5. Cuts on kinematic variables such as the track-photon
invariant mass m(trk, ￾), dR(trk,e) and dR(trk, ￾) are imposed to suppress background
tracks as much as possible.
More details of the event and track selection criteria are summarized in Appendix B.
All events and tracks passing these criteria are used for the LPET ID calibration.
78

=== PDF PAGE 90 ===
Strategy and method for performance measurement
The events and tracks passing the selection criteria above are dominated by background.
Z ! ee￾ consists only about 10% of the events passing the selection criteria. The re￾maining background events are mostly Z ! ee; one of the electrons fakes a photon.
Tracks passing the selection criteria are dominated by hadronic tracks originating from
underlying events for both Z ! ee￾ and Z ! ee events. These “fake” electron track con￾tributions can be inclusively considered as a single component. Therefore, the following
discussion categorizes the events into only two components: “true electron” tracks having
a low-pT electron track which originate from Z ! ee￾ and “fake electron” tracks which
do not.
To classify true and fake electron tracks in data, dR(trk, ￾) fit method has been in￾troduced. As shown in Fig. 5.17, the shapes of dR(trk, ￾) distributions largely di↵er for
true and fake electron tracks, because true electrons tend to be close to the photon, while
fake electrons, mostly hadrons from underlying events, are randomly positioned relative
to the selected photon for both Z ! ee and Z ! ee￾ processes. The main strategy
of the calibration is to fit these distributions for data using true and fake electron track
templates retrieved from MC. The fit is performed independently for tracks passing and
failing the LPET ID WP, respectively. Fig. 5.18 shows the post-fit distributions for tracks
passing and failing LPET ID WP 0.8.
Figure 5.17: Normalized distributions of dR(trk, ￾) for true and fake electron tracks. True electrons only
come from Z ! ee￾ events, while fake electron tracks also come from Z ! ee events.
The e￾ciency of LPET ID WP for true electron tracks "true
LPET ID can then be derived
by integrating the post-fit distributions for both pass and fail tracks, namely,
"true
LPET ID = Ntrue
pass
Ntrue
pass + Ntrue
fail
,
where Ntrue
pass and Ntrue
fail are numbers of pass and fail true electron tracks counted by the
integration. Since the LPET ID e￾ciency has strong dependence on track pT as already
shown in Fig. 5.15a, the fitting procedure is done for each track pT bin.
79

=== PDF PAGE 91 ===
(a) Post-fit distribution of dR(trk, ￾) for WP pass
tracks
(b) Post-fit distribution of dR(trk, ￾) for WP fail tracks
Figure 5.18: Post-fit distributions of dR(trk, ￾) for WP pass and fail tracks. The blue and red histograms
represent the fitted distributions of true and fake electron tracks respectively.
E￾ciency and scale factors
Fig. 5.19 shows the LPET ID WP e￾ciency and scale factors derived by dR(trk, ￾) fit
method. Large discrepancies between data and MC is observed, leading to small scale
factors. This is caused by poor MC modeling of input variables used for LPET ID DNN,
such as the TRT variables. By applying the scale factors to signal and background MC
tracks, these data/MC discrepancies of input variables are considered in an inclusive
manner. In addition, the scale factors for fake electron tracks, shown later in Appendix B
and not used in the analysis, have smaller values. Thus, the performance of LPET ID
WP for data is expected to be better than MC in terms of true e￾ciency and fake rate.
(a) LPET ID WP e￾ciency for true electron tracks (b) LPET ID WP scale factors for true electron tracks
Figure 5.19: LPET ID WP e￾ciency and scale factors derived by dR(trk, ￾) fit method. The data
e￾ciency is calculated by performing the fit for each track pT bin. Scale factors are calculated by taking
the ratio of data and MC e￾ciencies.

=== PDF PAGE 92 ===
5.2 Low-pT muon track
Similar to the low-pT electron track identification in Section 5.1, a new algorithm has been
developed for this search to identify low-pT muon tracks eluding the standard identification
algorithm described in Section 4.6. The contents summarized in this section are mainly
from Ref. [166].
A muon loses about 3 GeV of its energy when traversing the ATLAS calorimeters as a
minimum ionizing particle (MIP) [163]. Thus, standard muon identification described in
Section 4.6 has a lower pT threshold of 3 GeV as it requires hits in the muon spectrometers.
The basic idea of the new low-pT muon identification algorithm, called the Low-pT Calo
Tag (LCaT) muon algorithm, is to identify low-pT muons that do not reach the muon
spectrometers by exploiting the di↵erence of energy deposits in the calorimeters. Such
muons tend to stop within the outer layers of the calorimeters and behave as MIPs in those
layers, leaving little energy. On the other hand, background hadrons such as pions induce
hadronic showers and produce topo-clusters with large energy deposits. Fig. 5.20 shows
the energy deposit distributions of muons and pions in the first layer of Tile calorimeter.
0 0.5 1 1.5 2 2.5 3 3.5 4 4.5 5

0

0.12
|η|<1.6
 <5 GeV 
T 0.5 GeV < p
Tile Barrel First Layer 
single muon MC
single pion MC
Figure 5.20: Energy deposit distributions for muons and pions in the first layer of the Tile barrel [166].
Muons have a peak around 0.5 GeV corresponding to MIP energy deposit, while the energy of pions is
mostly distributed above that of a MIP.
The LCaT muon algorithm exploits track pT, track ⌘, and energy deposits in each of
the layer of the topo-cluster by inputting them in a deep neural network. Thus, track and
topo-cluster matching is required, which has been performed by a method similar to that
described in Section 5.1.1. The neural network has been trained using signal muons and
background particles in J/ ! µµ MC samples. The output muon likeliness score ranges
from 0 to 1 and is referred to as the LCaT DNN score.
A working point (WP) dedicated to the search for higgsinos has also been defined by
a neural-network-based method, similar to that mentioned in Sec. 5.1.2. The LCaT muon
DNN score, track pT, and track ⌘ are input into the LCaT muon WP DNN to output a
single WP score (LCaT muon WP score). The WP DNN has been trained with signal
muon tracks in higgsino MC and background tracks in W+jets MC. As shown in Fig. 5.21,
LCaT muon WP score larger than 0.6 is required in the analysis. Fig. 5.22 shows the

The calibration of the LCaT muon ID has been conducted by targeting the process of
J/ decay to two muons. In particular, tag and probe method was adopted to evaluate
the e￾ciency of the LCaT muon ID for both data and MC. Target events are required
to pass the single muon trigger, and the trigger matched muon is considered as the tag
muon. The distribution of invariant mass of the tag muon and the probe muon is fit using
crystal ball and quadratic functions to calculate the e￾ciency of LCaT muon ID for probe
muons. The e￾ciencies and scale factors obtained from this calibration method are shown
in Fig. 5.23. The scale factors are applied in this search to correct for the discrepancies
observed between data and MC, which are mostly within 10% for all pT and ⌘ regions.

To maximize sensitivity towards SUSY models having higgsinos with ￾m(˜￾0

O(1) GeV, dedicated event selection strategies utilizing the low-pT lepton track taggers,

strategies of the analysis, preselection and variables used for the definition of the Sig￾nal Region, neural network used to define the final discriminant, and the Signal Region
definition.
Two channels have been prepared for the analysis: electron channel and muon channel.
As the event selection strategies are similar for the two channels, they are described in a
parallel manner.

The signatures of these target processes could be observed in multiple detectors. First,
with the requirement of R-parity conservation, ˜￾0
1 are weakly interacting stable particles
and are likely to traverse the ATLAS detector without leaving any signals. These are
indirectly observed as missing transverse momentum pmiss
T (defined in Section 4.8), how￾ever, if two ˜￾0
1 are emitted back-to-back, the pmiss
T would cancel out and not be observed.
Therefore, an initial state radiation (ISR) jet is required in this analysis to boost the
target higgsino pair production system. In this case, the two ˜￾0
1 are emitted in the same
direction, opposite to the ISR jet, and could be observed as a high Emiss
T signature.
In addition, the features of the o↵-shell Z boson (Z⇤) help in enhancing the sensitivity.
The maximum value of the reconstructed Z⇤ mass equals the mass di↵erence ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1).
Moreover, since the system including the Z⇤ is boosted by the ISR jet, Z⇤ is emitted in
a direction close to the pmiss
T .
In previous studies [109, 167], the identification e￾ciencies of low-pT leptons were the
bottleneck of the search. In the target ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1) of a few GeV, typical pT of the emitted
leptons are a few GeV, however, pT thresholds of standard lepton identification are 4.5
GeV and 3 GeV for electrons and muons, respectively. This leads to very low acceptance
of the target signal processes, thus limiting the sensitivity.
To overcome this di￾culty, signal event e￾ciency is recovered by applying the newly
developed low-pT lepton track taggers: LPET ID to the electron channel and LCaT muon
ID to the muon channel. In particular, these new taggers are applied to only one of
the two leptons from Z⇤; standard identification is applied to the other lepton to realize
su￾cient background suppression. Hereinafter, the low-pT lepton identified with
the standard method is referred to as the “ID lepton”, while the other low￾pT lepton identified with the newly developed method is referred to as the
“lepton track”.
Furthermore, the kinematics of the two leptons from Z⇤ are exploited. The two leptons
are also boosted with the requirement of an ISR jet, which results in close-by ID lepton
and low-pT lepton track. The characteristics of the signal events are summarized in the
following list, and a schematic diagram of a typical signal event is shown in Fig. 6.2.
• Objects
– High Emiss
T
– High-pT ISR jet
– 1 low-pT ID lepton
– 1 low-pT lepton track
• Small angle between pmiss
T and Z⇤
• Small Z⇤ invariant mass
• Small angle between ID lepton and lepton track
The flow of the analysis, described later in detail, is as follows. First, event preselection
is applied to select events with a high-pT hadronic jet, large missing transverse momentum,
and one low-pT ID lepton. Next, track selection is performed to select a single candidate
low-pT lepton track from many of the background hadronic tracks originating mainly from
underlying events. Finally, the newly developed low-pT lepton track identification method
is applied to that candidate track to extract signal-like events.

=== PDF PAGE 99 ===
Figure 6.2: Schematic diagram of a signal event seen from the cross section of the ATLAS detector. An
ISR jet is required to boost the target higgsino pair production system. The Emiss
T and the two leptons
are objects originating from the boosted system.
6.2 Preselection and variables used to define the Sig￾nal Regions
Various requirements are imposed on distinctive variables of the event to discriminate
signals from background events and to define the Signal Regions (SRs).
6.2.1 Event kinematic variables
Events are preselected with the following criteria, summarized in Table 6.1.
Table 6.1: Event kinematics preselection applied to all events in SR for the electron channel and the
muon channel.

88

=== PDF PAGE 100 ===
Emiss
T requirements
Signal events are expected to emit high Emiss
T due to two boosted ˜￾0
1. Therefore, the
search uses data collected by inclusive Emiss
T triggers. The o✏ine Emiss
T is required
to be above 200 GeV, which ensures the trigger to be at its e￾ciency plateau and
be fully e￾cient, as described in Section 3.2.
Leading jet selection
In each event, the reconstructed jet with the highest pT is identified as the leading
jet. Leading jet pT is required to be larger than 100 GeV to boost the higgsino
pair production system. In addition, the leading jet is required to pass the Tight
jet cleaning working point, aforementioned in Section 4.4, for the reduction of non￾collision backgrounds.
Number of b-jets
B-veto is applied to suppress tt
¯ and single top backgrounds. It is required that
the number of b-jets with pT higher than 20 GeV be 0. The b-jets are identified
with DL1r algorithm with a condition corresponding to 85% e￾ciency, as already
mentioned in Section 4.4.
Number of leptons
Exactly one lepton is required to target signal events; one electron for the electron
channel and one muon for the muon channel, where the preselection requirements
have been described in Sections 4.5 and 4.6, respectively. This one lepton require￾ment assumes that the other lepton from Z⇤ decay fails the standard identification
criteria, because it is expected to have very low pT.
ID lepton selection
In addition to the preselection requirements, further criteria are required for the ID
lepton to realize su￾cient background suppression. Both the electron and the muon
are required to pass the Medium working point and to be low-pT: pT < 10 GeV.
Promptness of ID lepton
For the target ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1) of a few GeV, the lifetime of ˜￾0
2 is expected to be short
enough for the lepton to be prompt. Therefore, |z0 sin ✓| < 0.5 mm is required
for both leptons, and for the transverse impact parameter, |d0/￾(d0)| < 5 and
|d0/￾(d0)| < 3 are required for electrons and muons, respectively.
Isolation of ID lepton
Signal leptons originating from Z⇤ are expected to have little surrounding activity
compared to background leptons. Thus, the following “isolation” requirements are
imposed. Within a cone of dR =0.3, no charged tracks with pT > 1 GeV associated
to the hard-scatter vertex (excluding the track of the lepton itself) are allowed.
However, if the later mentioned low-pT lepton track candidate is within the cone,
its contribution is subtracted to avoid vetoing signal tracks.
6.2.2 Variables used for low-pT lepton track preselection
Candidate tracks, which the newly developed low-pT lepton track ID will be applied to,
are preselected by the following criteria. Events with no candidate tracks fail the event
89

=== PDF PAGE 101 ===
selection and are not used in the analysis. The track preselection criteria are summarized
in Table 6.2.
Table 6.2: Track preselection applied to low-pT lepton candidate tracks for the electron channel and the
muon channel.
Variable Electron channel Muon channel
Quality WP TightPrimary
Charge Opposite of ID lepton
pT [GeV] [0.5, 5] [1, 5]
|⌘| < 2.5 < 1.6
|z0 sin ✓| [mm] < 0.5
|d0| [mm] < 0.5
Isolation No activities within dR =0.3

￾z0(lep,trk) [mm] < 0.30 < 0.26
Track quality and basic kinematic selections
First, the tracks must pass the TightPrimary working point described in Section 4.1.
As the signal track comes from the Z⇤ decay, its charge is required to be opposite of
that of the ID lepton. The required track pT ranges are 0.5–5 GeV and 1–5 GeV for
the electron and muon channel, respectively. For the track position, |⌘| < 2.5 and
|⌘| < 1.6 are required for the electron and muon channel, respectively. These pT
and ⌘ requirements correspond to the LPET ID and LCaT muon ID target regions.
In addition, impact parameter requirements are applied to both channels to target
prompt lepton signals and to reject pileup track contributions: |z0 sin ✓| < 0.5 mm
and |d0| < 0.5 mm. For track isolation, a requirement similar to that of the ID
lepton isolation is imposed: no charged tracks associated to the hard-scatter vertex
are allowed within a cone of dR =0.3, with the exception of the ID lepton to avoid
vetoing signal tracks.
￾￾(pmiss
T ,Z⇤)
￾￾(pmiss
T ,Z⇤) is the angular di↵erence between the Emiss
T and Z⇤, defined by
￾￾(pmiss
T ,Z⇤
)= |￾(pmiss
T ) ￾ ￾(Z⇤
)|
in which ￾(pmiss
T ) and ￾(Z⇤) are the directions of missing transverse momentum and
Z⇤ in ￾. It is defined to be the absolute value of the angular di↵erence, ranging
from 0 to ⇡. For signals, ￾￾(pmiss
T ,Z⇤) is expected to be small since both Emiss
T
and Z⇤ originate from the higgsino pair production system boosted by the ISR
jet. This variable is not used for the track preselection but is used as one of the
inputs of the event selection neural network described later in Section 6.3. It also
plays a significant role in the definition of regions used to validate the background
estimation method, described later in Section 7.2.
90

=== PDF PAGE 102 ===
m(Z⇤)
The mass of the reconstructed o↵-shell Z boson m(Z⇤) has a peaking distribution
with its maximum value at ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1). Therefore, the distribution of m(Z⇤) de￾pends on ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1). m(Z⇤) < 5 GeV is required for all scenarios since ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1)
of the target scenarios are below 5 GeV. In addition, 3–3.2 GeV is rejected to veto
tracks coming from J/ decays.
dR(lep,trk)
dR(lep,trk) is the distance between the ID lepton and the lepton track defined by
dR(lep,trk) = q
(⌘lep ￾ ⌘trk)2 +(￾lep ￾ ￾trk)2.
dR(lep,trk) also depends on ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1); it is smaller for smaller mass splitting
values. This is because Z⇤ with lighter mass tends to be more boosted, and the dis￾tribution of m(Z⇤) depends on ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1). dR(lep,trk) < 1.4 is required to target
scenarios with mass splitting values below 5 GeV. On top of this, dR(lep,trk) > 0.1
is imposed to veto very close-by tracks associated to the ID lepton.
￾d0(lep,trk) and ￾z0(lep,trk)
The di↵erences of impact parameters between the ID lepton and the lepton track
￾d0(lep,trk) and ￾z0(lep,trk) defined by
￾d0(lep,trk) = |d0(lep) ￾ d0(trk)|,
￾z0(lep,trk) = |z0(lep) ￾ z0(trk)|
are also used to preselect candidate tracks. The d0 and z0 of the lepton and the
track should be close since they both come from the same Z⇤ decay. For the electron
channel, ￾d0(lep,trk) < 0.25 mm and ￾z0(lep,trk) < 0.30 mm are required. For the
muon channel, ￾d0(lep,trk) < 0.10 mm and ￾z0(lep,trk) < 0.26 mm are required.
6.3 Parameterized neural network for event selection
The event topology of signal events has large dependence on the mass splitting of the
higgsinos, namely ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1). For very small ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1), the leptons emitted from ˜￾0
2 !
￾˜0
1Z⇤ decay have very low pT, and the two ˜￾0
1 tend to become more collimated, leading
to higher Emiss
T values. In this case, variables such as dR(lep,trk) and ￾￾(pmiss
T ,Z⇤) have
very small values. For larger ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1), the leptons have higher pT, leading to lower
Emiss
T , and therefore, larger dR(lep,trk) and ￾￾(pmiss
T ,Z⇤).
To consider these dependencies and to classify signal and background events in a dy￾namic manner, parameterized neural network (pNN) [111] is introduced. Fig. 6.3 shows
a schematic diagram of pNN. By choosing ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1) as the physics parameter ✓ (here￾inafter referred to as pNN-✓), it is possible to realize ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1)-dependent event selection
with a single network model. This also simplifies the training procedure, as pNN has the
ability to smoothly interpolate between ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1) used for training, thus giving the pos￾sibility to explore various mass splitting scenarios with limited number of training samples.
This section describes the inputs, the model and its training, and the performance of the
pNN designed for event selection.

=== PDF PAGE 103 ===

x1
x2
fa(x1,x2)
= a
x1
x2
f(x1,x2, )
x1
x2
fb(x1,x2)
= b
Fig. 1 Left, individual networks with input features (x1, x2), each
trained with examples with a single value of some parameter θ = θa , θb.
The individual networks are purely functions of the input features. Per￾formance for intermediate values of θ is not optimal nor does it nec￾essarily vary smoothly between the networks. Right, a single network
trained with input features(x1, x2) as well as an input parameter θ; such
a network is trained with examples at several values of the parameter θ
tion of θ¯ introduces additional considerations in the training
procedure. While traditionally the training only requires the
conditional distribution of x¯ given θ¯ (which is predicted by
the theory and detector simulation), now the training data
has some implicit prior distribution over θ¯ as well (which is
arbitrary). When the network is used in practice it will be
to predict y conditional on both x¯ and θ¯, so the distribution
of θ¯ used for training is only relevant in how it affects the
quality of the resulting parameterized network – it does not
imply that the resulting inference is Bayesian. In the studies
presented below, we simply use equal sized samples for a few
discrete values of θ¯. Another issue is that some or all of the
components of θ¯ may not be meaningful for a particular target
class. For instance, the mass of a new particle is not meaning￾ful for the background training examples. In what follows,
we randomly assign values to those components of θ¯ accord￾ing to the same distribution used for the signal class. In the
examples studied below, the networks have enough general￾ization capacity and the training sets are large enough that
the resulting parameterized classifier performs well without
any tuning of the training procedure. However, the robust￾ness of the resulting parameterized classifier to the implicit
distribution of θ¯ in the training sample will in general depend
on the generalization capacity of the classifier, the number of
training examples, the physics encoded in the distributions
p(x¯|θ¯, y), and how much those distributions change with θ¯.
3 Toy example
As a demonstration for a simple toy problem, we construct a
parameterized network which has a single input feature x and
a single parameter θ. The network, with one hidden layer of
three nodes and sigmoid activation functions, is trained using
labeled examples where examples with label 0 are drawn
from a uniform background and examples with label 1 are
Fig. 2 Top training samples in which the signal is drawn from a Gaus￾sian and the background is uniform. Bottom, neural network response
as a function of the value of the input feature x, for various choices of
the input parameter θ; note that the single parameterized network has
seen no training examples for θ = −1.5, −0.5, 0.5, 1.5
drawn from a Gaussian with mean θ and width σ = 0.25.
Training samples are generated with θ = −2, −1, 0, 1, 2;
see Fig. 2a.
As shown in Fig. 2, this network generalizes the solu￾tion and provides reasonable output even for values of the
parameter where it was given no examples. Note that the
response function has the same shape for these values (θ =
−1.5, −0.5, 0.5, 1.5) as for values where training data was
provided, indicating that the network has successfully param￾eterized the solution. The signal-background classification
accuracy is as good for values where training data exist as it
is for values where training data does not.
4 1D physical example
A natural physical case is the application to the search for new
particle of unknown mass. As an example, we consider the
search for a new particle X which decays to tt
¯. We treat the
123
Figure 6.3: Schematic diagram of pNN [111]. In the case where input features x1 and x2 depend on
the physics parameter ✓, with conventional neural networks, a dedicated neural network for each ✓ value
would be necessary to realize optimized performance (left). Instead with parameterized neural networks,
only a single network is necessary to achieve the purpose, simply by using ✓ as one of the inputs to the
network (right).
6.3.1 Input variables
The input variables of the pNN are summarized in Table 6.3. The same set of variables
are used for both electron and muon channels, because they are expected to have the
same event topology. However, a separate network is prepared for each channel, since the
kinematic requirements of leptons and tracks di↵er as mentioned in Section 6.2.
Out of the 16 input variables (excluding pNN-✓), lepton pT, track pT, Emiss
T , leading jet
pT, m(Z⇤), dR(lep,trk), H(lep,trk)/Emiss
T , mT(lep,Emiss
T ), ￾￾(pmiss
T ,Z⇤), and ￾￾(j1,Z⇤)
have significant dependencies on ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1), thus having high importance. Normalized
distributions of these input variables are shown in Fig. 6.4 and Fig. 6.5 for the electron
channel and the muon channel, respectively. The topology di↵erences between signal and
background events tend to become more significant for smaller ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1).
6.3.2 Model and training
Fig. 6.6 shows the overall structure of the pNN model developed for event selection. This
model is used for both electron and muon channels. Based on ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1), which is
the pNN-✓, and 16 kinematic variables, the algorithm outputs a signal event likeliness
score (hereinafter referred to as pNN score) ranging from 0 to 1. All of the inputs are
standardized following Eq. 5.1.
Since the kinematic requirements for leptons and tracks di↵er for the electron and
muon channel, the training of pNN has been done separately for each channel using the
same pNN model. To ensure enough statistics, higgsino production MC events have
been used inclusively in terms of mass points and production channels as signal training
samples. W+jets MC events have been used as background training samples. pNN-✓
of background training samples are assigned randomly so that its distribution matches
that of the signal training samples. All training samples are required to pass the event

6.3.4 Application
In the case where a simple neural network is used for a search analysis, the neural network
model is trained and optimized for each mass point. On the other hand, pNN has a single
model trained to perform for each of the mass points, but with additional degrees of
freedom, which could be adjusted manually. In the analysis, the degree of freedom is the
pNN-✓, corresponding to the ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1).
The pNN in this search analysis is trained with pNN-✓ = 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4 and 5 GeV. The
same values are used for the search by fixing the pNN-✓ to each value. Therefore, there
are six models to choose from for each mass point. Straightforwardly, the best performing
model for each mass point should be the one having the same pNN-✓, however, this might
not always be the case due to fluctuations. Hence, the best performing pNN-✓ is chosen
for each mass point by comparing the final CLs values obtained from statistical analysis
described later in Section 9.
6.4 Signal Region definition
Fig. 6.10 shows the flow of this search analysis. Event and track preselection requirements
defined in Section 6.2 are first applied. Next, a single track with the highest pNN score
is selected for each event. This is performed by reconstructing the Z⇤ candidate and
applying the pNN with each of the preselected tracks. After the track selection, cuts on
￾￾(pmiss
T ,Z⇤) and the pNN score are applied to select signal-like events. The regions that
fail these requirements are used as Validation Regions (VRs) of the background estimation
method. Finally, low-pT lepton track ID is applied to the selected track with dedicated
WPs. The regions passing the low-pT lepton track ID requirement are used as Signal
Regions (SRs). The definition of SRs is discussed in this section.
Figure 6.10: Diagram of the overall analysis flow. The basic flow is common to the electron and muon
channels, while the detailed requirements slightly di↵er for each channel.
98

=== PDF PAGE 110 ===
The general idea of SR definition is to maximize the sensitivity towards signals by
choosing regions where the signal contribution is relatively high compared to SM back￾ground contributions. This is achieved by applying two independent selections on the
main variables of the analysis: the pNN score and the low-pT lepton track ID score. By
first selecting events with high pNN scores, background events having non-signal-like event
topology are vetoed. Then by selecting events having a track with high low-pT lepton
track ID WP score, significant background suppression is achieved by rejecting hadronic
and fake tracks, which are the main background track components in the targeted regime.
Table 6.5 summarizes the selection criteria of SRs for the electron channel and the
muon channel. The requirement on ￾￾(pmiss
T ,Z⇤) is imposed to keep the orthogonality
with Validation Regions defined for the background estimation method and is common to
both channels. The requirements on the pNN score and low-pT lepton track ID WP score
have been optimized to realize maximum sensitivity with expected background assuming
20% uncertainty.
Table 6.5: Summary of the selection criteria of SR.
Variable Electron channel Muon channel
￾￾(pmiss
T ,Z⇤) < 1
pNN score > 0.95
Low-pT lepton track ID LPET WP score > 0.8 LCaT muon WP score > 0.6
99

=== PDF PAGE 111 ===
7. Methodology of Background Esti￾mation
In this section, the main sources of background events are presented, followed by the
details of the strategy of estimating the background contribution in the Signal Region.
7.1 Background components
All SM processes with a high-pT jet and a boosted system with neutrinos in the final
state could pass the leading jet and Emiss
T selection of this search. The requirements
for the ID lepton, which the standard lepton identification is applied to, are relatively
tight, hence the ID lepton is expected to be a real lepton in most cases. On the other
hand, the requirements for the low-pT lepton track, which the newly developed low-pT
lepton track identification method is applied to, are much looser; its main contribution is
hadronic/fake tracks. Since the tracks are required to be associated with the hard-scatter
vertex and have no surrounding activities, most of the background tracks are expected to
be originating from underlying events. Some of these tracks could potentially imitate the
signal lepton track originating from Z⇤.
Considering that the target event topology requires a high-pT hadronic jet, large miss￾ing momentum, and a single lepton, the most probable background process is W+jets,
where the W decays leptonically. Fig. 7.1 shows the diagram of W+jets process. In this
process, the true lepton originating from the W will likely be identified as the ID lepton,
and the neutrino will likely result in high Emiss
T signature. As already mentioned, the
hadronic/fake tracks would fake the low-pT lepton track in this case. Moreover, there
could also be small contributions from true leptonic tracks. Z+jets, diboson, and other
processes which could possibly have two or more true leptons in their final states, could
contribute as “true lepton” backgrounds. The dominant background with a “fake low-pT
lepton track” is estimated by a data-driven method described in Section 7.2, whereas the
subdominant background with a “true low-pT lepton track” is estimated directly from
MC simulation as described in Section 7.3. A semi data-driven method is therefore used
for the background estimation of the analysis.
100

=== PDF PAGE 112 ===
Figure 7.1: Diagram of W+jets process. This process is the main background of this search.
7.2 Data-driven estimation of 1L + fake lepton track
background
A data-driven method has been adopted to estimate the dominant hadronic/fake track
background in the SR due to the following reasons:
• The reliability of MC simulation modeling of low-pT hadronic/fake tracks is not
fully understood,
• Systematic uncertainties for the MC modeling of fake low-pT lepton track would not
have to be considered, allowing for a more accurate estimation.
The general idea of this method is to measure and apply the pass-to-fail ratios or the
“transfer factors” of the low-pT lepton track ID in certain Control Regions (CRs), having
di↵erent kinematics compared to the SR. The validity of the method is verified in multiple
Validation Regions (VRs). This transfer factor method is compatible with the analysis
flow visualized in Fig. 6.10 and is common to both electron and muon channels.
Although the method mentioned above is expected to estimate nearly 90% of the total
background in the SR, it has been observed in VRs that the above method does not fully
consider relatively small contributions from true leptons to the low-pT lepton track. These
contributions are estimated using MC simulations as discussed later in Section 7.3. For
the estimation of fake lepton track background, the contributions from “true leptons” are
subtracted from data in each of the CRs to properly measure the hadronic/fake track
transfer factors.
101

=== PDF PAGE 113 ===
CR definitions and estimation procedures
Here, the definitions of the CRs used for measuring and applying the low-pT lepton track
ID transfer factors are described. The definitions are identical for both electron and muon
channels.
Figure 7.2: Kinematic plane diagram used for region definitions. In terms of kinematics, the SR, CRs,
and VRs are defined by imposing cuts on the pNN score and ￾￾(pmiss
T ,Z⇤).
Fig. 7.2 shows the kinematic plane used to define the SR, CRs, and VRs. The SR,
which has already been defined in Section 6.4, is within the ↵-region, where pNN score
threshold p is defined in Table 6.5 for each channel. It must be noted that the kinematic
regions defined here do not consider the low-pT lepton track ID requirements in Table 6.5;
therefore, the SR is defined by the region passing the low-pT lepton track ID WP within
the ↵-region.
Since the low-pT lepton track ID and the event selection pNN are defined independently
of each other, the low pNN score region, dominated by SM background, is a valid region
for measuring the transfer factors. In particular, the kinematic ￾-region, with pNN scores
lower than 0.4, is used. Within this ￾-region, the region passing the low-pT lepton track
ID WP requirements (WP pass region) in Table 6.5 is defined as CR-A, and the region
failing the WP requirements (WP fail region) is defined as CR-B. The transfer factors are
then measured by taking the ratio of the number of events belonging to CR-A to that of
CR-B. To consider the e↵ects of indirect relations between the low-pT lepton track ID and
the pNN through the track kinematics, the transfer factors are measured separately for
each of the track pT bins listed in Table 7.1. This track pT binned transfer factor method
is capable of estimating the pNN score shape in the WP pass region.
The measured transfer factors are then applied to the WP fail region within the
kinematic ↵-region, defined as CR-C. This method is expected to correctly estimate the
pNN score shape in the SR, and its validity has been checked with MC simulated samples.
Fig. 7.3 shows a schematic diagram of the background estimation method, summarizing
the definition of each CR.
102

=== PDF PAGE 114 ===
Table 7.1: List of track pT bins used for low-pT lepton track ID transfer factor measurement. The electron
channel has an additional bin compared to the muon channel, since the target track pT range is lower.
Track pT bin name Electron channel Muon channel
Track pT bin A [0.5, 1.0] GeV -
Track pT bin B [1.0, 1.5] GeV
Track pT bin C [1.5, 2.0] GeV
Track pT bin D [2.0, 3.0] GeV
Track pT bin E [3.0, 5.0] GeV
Figure 7.3: Schematic diagram of the background estimation method. CR-A and CR-B are used to
measured the transfer factors (pass-to-fail ratio) of the low-pT lepton track ID WP. The transfer factors
are binned by the track pT to correctly estimate the pNN score shape in the SR. The measured transfer
factors are then applied to CR-C to estimate the background in the SR.
103

=== PDF PAGE 115 ===
7.3 MC estimation of 1L + true lepton track back￾ground
As briefly mentioned in the previous section, the subdominant contribution of 1L +
true lepton track background is estimated using MC simulation. These contributions
are considered by adding them directly to the data-driven estimated fake lepton track
contribution in the SRs and VRs. The considered background MC samples are listed
in Table 3.3, and true lepton tracks are selected by matching with corresponding truth
particles. Processes which have two or more prompt leptons, such as Z+jets and diboson
production, tend to have small but non-negligible contributions. The e↵ects of non￾prompt leptons from processes such as dijet production and ￾+jets have also been checked
but were found to be negligible.
7.4 Validation strategy
The background estimation method described in Sections 7.2 and 7.3 must be validated
by checking the agreement of estimated background and data in certain VRs. This is
done in multiple VRs, having su￾ciently low signal contamination. The VRs are used to
evaluate the non-closure systematics of the background estimation method later described
in Section 8.
Since the estimation method assumes that the transfer factors measured in the low
pNN score region are also valid in the high pNN score region, this validity must be
checked with a dedicated VR. This is done within the kinematic ￾-region in Fig. 7.2,
defined by pNN score > 0.4 and ￾￾(pmiss
T ,Z⇤) > 1.4. Although the SR is defined by
￾￾(pmiss
T ,Z⇤) < 1.0, the cut threshold for the ￾-region has been raised to 1.4 to suppress
signal contamination. Fig. 7.4 shows the schematic diagram of the validation using the
kinematic ￾-region. By applying the measured transfer factors to CR-highLarge, which
corresponds to the WP fail region within the ￾-region, the number of events in VR￾highLarge, defined by WP pass region within the ￾-region, is estimated.
Additional VRs are considered to verify the validity of the background estimation
method in the ↵-region. This is performed by flipping the charge or the flavor relations
between the ID lepton and the low-pT lepton track. For the SR shown in Fig. 7.3, the
ID lepton and the low-pT lepton track are required to have opposite signs (OS) and be of
the same flavor (SF), namely, electrons for the electron channel and muons for the muon
channel. For additional VRs, the ID lepton and the low-pT lepton track are requied to
have the same sign (SS) or di↵erent flavors (DF), with other requirements set identically
to those of the SR. For these two VRs and their corresponding CRs, the pNN score
thresholds for ↵-regions are lowered to 0.4 to increase statistics.
For the same sign VR, defined as VR-SS, the transfer factors are measured and applied
within the same sign region; therefore, additional CRs named CR-SS-A, CR-SS-B, and
CR-SS-C are defined in correspondence with the three CRs shown in Fig. 7.3. Fig. 7.5
shows the schematic diagram of the validation using VR-SS. The estimated background
in VR-SS is compared to data for validation.
For the di↵erent flavor VR, defined as VR-DF, the transfer factors are measured and
applied within the di↵erent flavor region. For the electron channel, an ID muon and a low￾104

=== PDF PAGE 116 ===
Figure 7.4: Schematic diagram of the background estimation validation in VR-highLarge. The transfer
factors measured in the low pNN score region are applied to CR-highLarge to estimate the number of
events in VR-highLarge.
Figure 7.5: Schematic diagram of the background estimation validation in VR-SS. CR-SS-A and CR-SS￾B are used to measured the same sign transfer factors (TF-SS) of the low-pT lepton track ID WP. The
transfer factors are binned by the track pT using the same binning as the OS-SF regions. The measured
transfer factors are then applied to CR-SS-C to estimate the background in VR-SS.
105

=== PDF PAGE 117 ===
pT electron track are considered to measure the transfer factors of LPET ID. For the muon
channel, an ID electron and a low-pT muon track are considered to measure the transfer
factors of LCaT muon ID. Therefore, additional CRs named CR-DF-A, CR-DF-B, and
CR-DF-C are defined in correspondence with the three CRs shown in Fig. 7.3. Fig. 7.6
shows the schematic diagram of the validation using VR-DF. The estimated background
in VR-DF is compared to data for validation.
Figure 7.6: Schematic diagram of the background estimation validation in VR-DF. CR-DF-A and CR￾DF-B are used to measured the di↵erent flavor transfer factors (TF-DF) of the low-pT lepton track ID
WP. The transfer factors are binned by the track pT using the same binning as the OS-SF region. The
measured transfer factors are then applied to CR-DF-C to estimate the background in VR-DF.
7.5 Summary of CR and VR definitions
In summary, there are three VRs and 10 CRs for each channel as listed in Table 7.2.
Three CRs (CR-A/B/C) are necessary for estimating the SR background: two for TF
measurement and one for TF application. The remaining seven CRs are dedicated to
validation: one (CR-highLarge) for VR-highLarge, three (CR-A/B/C-SS) for VR-SS, and
three (CR-A/B/C-DF) for VR-DF. It should be again noted that the contributions from
true lepton tracks are subtracted from CRs before measuring the transfer factors and are
added to SRs and VRs afterwards.

7.6 Estimation results in the Signal Region
By adding the data-driven estimated fake lepton track contribution and MC estimated
true lepton track contribution, the total SM background in the SR is estimated for the
electron and muon channels for each pNN-✓, as mentioned in Section 6.3.4. This is done by
applying the systematic uncertainties later described in Section 8 and using the statistical
fit framework described in Section 9. Table 7.3 and Table 7.4 summarize the estimated
event yields in the SR for the electron channel and the muon channel, respectively.
