8. Systematic Uncertainties
This section describes the systematic uncertainties considered in this search: experimen￾tal uncertainties, theoretical uncertainties, and analysis specific uncertainties. As the
dominant background is the fake lepton track contribution estimated by the data-driven
method, the non-closure uncertainty on that component is the most dominant source of
the systematic uncertainty. Although contributions of background MC samples are rel￾atively small and their systematic uncertainties should have little impact on the results,
they have been evaluated to a certain extent.
8.1 Experimental uncertainties
Experimental uncertainties are considered for the MC samples to account for the mis￾modeling in detector responses and imperfections in calibrations. The considered exper￾imental uncertainties are described below. Experimental uncertainties for the signal MC
samples have been implemented straightforwardly by handling each of the uncertainties
as separate nuisance parameters in the statistical analysis framework later described in
Section 9. On the other hand, for background MC samples used for estimating the true
lepton contribution, the experimental uncertainties have been handled as a flat 50% en￾velope systematic of all uncertainties described below and implemented to the statistical
framework as a single nuisance parameter. This is to prevent fit instabilities in the frame￾work, caused by low MC statistics. As the true lepton track contribution accounts for
only about 10% of the SR background, its uncertainties are of second order and have
minimal impact on the total uncertainty.
Electrons
• Energy resolution: up/down variations in the resolution of the electron energy.
• Energy scale: up/down variations in the scale of the electron energy.
• Reconstruction e￾ciency: up/down variations in the error of the electron recon￾struction scale factors.
• Identification e￾ciency: up/down variations in the error of the electron identifica￾tion working-point scale factors.
• Isolation e￾ciency: up/down variations in the error of the electron isolation working￾point scale factors.
109

=== PDF PAGE 121 ===
Muons
• Inner Detector smearing: up/down variations in the smearing of the muon tracks
in the Inner Detector.
• Muon spectrometer smearing: up/down variations in the smearing of the muon
tracks in the muon spectrometers.
• Momentum scale: up/down variations in the muon momentum scale.
• Sagitta resolution: up/down charge-dependent variations in the scale of the momen￾tum and its statistical error associated to the charge-dependent corrections.
• Reconstruction e￾ciency: up/down variations in the statistical and systematic er￾rors on the muon reconstruction scale factors.
• Isolation e￾ciency: up/down variations in statistical and systematic errors on the
muon isolation working-point scale factors.
• Track-to-vertex association (TTVA) e￾ciency: up/down variations in statistical and
systematic errors on the muon TTVA scale factors.
Jets
• Jet energy resolution: up/down variations in the smearing of the MC jets to the
data to correct the MC resolution. These uncertainties are evaluated from dijet
events and zero bias data using random cones.
• Jet energy scale: up/down variations in the scale of the jet momentum. These uncer￾tainties are derived from the dijet eta-intercalibration across eta, Z(! ee, µµ)+jets,
￾+jets and multijet balance, and the propagation of single particles in high-pT
regimes.
• Jet vertex tagger: up/down variations accounting for the residual contamination
from pileup jets in the central region.
• Flavor tagging e￾ciency: up/down variations in the error on the scale factors used
to correct the simulation-over-data flavor tagging rate.
Missing transverse momentum
• Soft term resolution: one-sided variations of the Emiss
T track soft term resolution,
parallel and perpendicular to the hard term.
• Soft term scale: up/down variations in the Emiss
T track soft term scale, obtained by
scaling the soft term magnitude in the direction of phard
T .
110

=== PDF PAGE 122 ===
Others
• Pileup: up/down variations of the pileup weight used to reweight nominal MC
predictions to reproduce the correct distribution of the number of pp collisions per
bunch crossing in data.
• Track impact parameter resolution: one-sided variations on the transverse and lon￾gitudinal impact parameter resolutions to account for any di↵erences with respect to
data. These uncertainties are obtained by replacing the nominal impact parameter
with a random value taken from a Gaussian distribution, whose central value is the
original MC impact parameter and whose sigma is taken from dedicated smearing
maps from studies on MC/data resolution di↵erences.
8.2 Theoretical uncertainties
Theoretical uncertainties for signal and background MC samples have been evaluated
to consider generator-level modeling uncertainties. Note that for the background MC
samples, all theoretical uncertainties have been considered only for diboson and V +jets
samples, which are the dominant components of the true lepton track contribution. Un￾certainties of other background processes have negligible impact and are hence not con￾sidered.
The following variations of QCD renormalization and factorization scales and parton
distribution functions (PDFs) have been taken into account for both signal and back￾ground MC samples. These uncertainties are combined by taking their quadratic sum.
QCD renormalization and factorization scales
Renormalization and factorization scales (µR and µF ) are used to estimate the un￾certainties originating from truncated higher orders in the perturbative expansion
of the partonic cross-section corrections. The uncertainties are estimated by varying
µR and µF by a factor of two resulting in 7-point variations of {µR,µF } = {0.5, 0.5},
{1.0, 0.5}, {0.5, 1.0}, {1.0, 1.0}, {2.0, 1.0}, {1.0, 2.0}, {2.0, 2.0}. 3-point variations
of {µR,µF } = {0.5, 0.5}, {1.0, 1.0}, {2.0, 2.0} are used when the 7-point variations
are unavailable at generator-level. The envelope of the 7-point (3-point) variations
are taken as the overall uncertainty.
Parton Distribution Function (PDF)
PDFs at a given scale (Q) are determined by fitting a given functional form to
data. The PDF at di↵erent energy scales in the MC generator is estimated by
DGLAP equations [168–170]. Therefore, experimental uncertainties in the used
dataset and uncertainty on the functional form for PDF fits are sources of uncer￾tainties. Truncated higher order calculations of DGLAP equations could also be
sources of uncertainties, however, they are considered by varying the renormaliza￾tion and factorization scales as mentioned above.
Additionally, the following uncertainties are considered, depending on the signal and
background processes.
111

=== PDF PAGE 123 ===
Cross section uncertainty (for signal MC only)
The theoretical uncertainty on the cross section for signal MC is considered. Similar
to the nominal cross section value, its uncertainty is also evaluated to NLO+NLL
accuracy using Resummino [130–135]. The relative uncertainty is less than 5% for
all mass points.
Strong coupling constant (↵S) (for background MC only)
↵S is determined experimentally and quoted at the Z mass scale (Q = mZ). ↵S
at di↵erent energy scales in the MC generators are predicted by renormalization
group equations (RGEs), although the calculations are truncated at a given order.
Therefore, experimental errors of ↵S and missing higher order calculations of RGEs
need to be accounted as uncertainties of ↵S. PDF sets with di↵erent ↵S values are
used to estimate the uncertainties.
8.3 Analysis specific uncertainties
Additional uncertainties considered for this analysis are described below.
Low-pT lepton track ID scale factor uncertainty
As described in Sections 5.1 and 5.2, low-pT lepton track ID scale factors are ap￾plied to MC samples to consider the behavior di↵erences between data and MC
measured by the calibration. Up/down variations are applied by accounting for
the uncertainties originating from the calibration, which primarily depends on the
statistical uncertainties of the calibration data and the parametrization by track
pT. Uncertainties depending on track ⌘ and other parameters have been omitted
for simplicity, because the impact of this scale factor uncertainty on the total back￾ground estimation is small, as this uncertainty is only applied to the subdominant
true lepton track background component.
Non-closure uncertainty for background estimation
The non-closure of the background estimation method is expected to originate
mainly from the uncertainties in the extrapolation of pNN scores using track pT
binned transfer factors, as the transfer factors could have dependecies on other vari￾ables. These uncertainties are evaluated by comparing the data and estimation in
VRs and are applied as flat systematic uncertainties to the fake lepton track com￾ponents in the SR. As a result of the evaluation, 15% and 40% flat systematics are
applied to electron and muon channels, respectively.
CR statistical uncertainty
The statistical uncertainties of data in the CRs used for estimating the fake lep￾ton background in the SR also have some impact. These have been considered
as normalization factor and transfer factor uncertainties in the statistical analysis
framework, later described in Section 9.
MC statistical uncertainty
The statistical uncertainties of the signal and background MC samples have been
considered in all regions. The impact of the statistical uncertainties of the back￾ground MC samples is minimal, since the CRs are dominated by fake lepton contri￾bution, and only a fraction of events are truly leptonic in the SR.
112

=== PDF PAGE 124 ===
8.4 Impact on background estimation
Tables 8.1 and 8.2 show the impact of the systematic uncertainties on the estimated back￾ground for the electron channel and muon channel, respectively. The numbers have been
obtained by performing statistical fits explained later in Section 9. Since the behavior is
similar for all pNN-✓ configurations, numbers for pNN-✓ = 2 GeV are shown as typical
examples. For both channels, the non-closure uncertainty on the fake lepton track back￾ground is the most dominant. Other uncertainties on the fake lepton background, such
as the transfer factor and normalization factor uncertainties, have non-negligible e↵ects.
On the other hand, as the contribution from true lepton track component is small, corre￾sponding uncertainties such as the 50% envelope experimental uncertainty, MC statistical
uncertainty, and the low-pT lepton track ID scale factor uncertainty, have relatively small
impact on the estimated background.

9. Statistical Analysis
Statistical fits have been performed to estimate the number of events in the SRs and test
its compatibility under various hypotheses. This has been done with a profile likelihood
ratio approach using the HistFitter package [171]. This chapter describes the definition
of the likelihood function and the methodology of hypothesis testing.
9.1 Likelihood function
The likelihood L of the electron or muon channel analysis is defined as the product
of Poisson distributions of the number of events in the SR and CRs and the Gaussian
probability densities of the systematic uncertainties:
L(n|µsig, s, b, ✓)= PSR ⇥ PCR-A ⇥ PCR-B ⇥ PCR-C ⇥ Csyst, (9.1)
where Pi is the Poisson measurement in region i, and Csyst is the product of Gaussian
probability distributions of all considered systematic uncertainties. Poisson measurement
Pi is represented by the observed number of events ni and the expected number of ￾i in
region i:
Pi = P(ni|￾i)= ￾ni
i

where the expected number of events ￾i is expressed as
￾i(µsig,si, b, ✓)= µsig · si(✓)+ X

Each of the parameters in Eq. (9.3) are defined as below:
• µsig is the normalization factor of the signal process and is referred to as the signal
strength.
• si is the expected number of signal events in region i, obtained from MC simulation.
• ↵ represents the type of fake lepton background. As the fake lepton background
is estimated with binning of track pT as mentioned in Section 7.2, the summation
runs over the track pT bins listed in Table 7.1.
• bi,↵ is the expected number of fake lepton background events, estimated by the data￾driven method explained in Section 7.2. The data-driven estimation is performed
by a simultaneous fit within the statistical fit framework.
115

• ￾ represents the physics process of true lepton background MC.
• bi,￾ is the expected number of true lepton background events in region i for physics
process ￾, obtained from MC simulation.
• ✓ represents the set of nuisance parameters, in particular, all of the considered
systematic uncertainties mentioned in Section 8.
The systematic uncertainty factor Csyst is written as
Csyst = Y

where S is the set of all considered systematic uncertainties, and G(✓j ) is the standard
Gaussian distribution of the systematic uncertainty ✓j . As the mean of the distribution
is fixed to 0, ✓ = ±1 corresponds to ±1￾ variations. Hereinafter, the likelihood will be
denoted as L(µsig, ✓), as the number of signal and background events are functions of ✓.

For the analysis of this search, background-only hypothesis with µsig = 0 is first con￾sidered to predict the number of background events in the SR for both electron and muon
channels. If there is no significant excess of observed data compared to the prediction,
model-dependent and model-independent fits are performed.

done using the CLs method [173]. In particular, CLs+b, CLb, and CLs are defined as

In this method, the signal hypothesis is considered to be excluded at 95% confidence level
if CLs < 0.05. This is the standard criterion for setting exclusion limits in the ATLAS
experiment and is used in this analysis.
The model-independent fit aims to set upper limits on the number of events coming
from physics beyond the SM. This is performed by scanning over the signal strength and
calculating the p-value with Eq. (9.7) to find the number of expected and observed signal

9.3 Fit configuration
All of the systematic uncertainties described in Section 8 are implemented in the statis￾tical fit framework as nuisance parameters. To speed up the fitting procedure, nuisance
parameters are pruned when their deviation from the nominal value is less than 1%.
Simultaneous fit for data-driven estimation of fake lepton tracks
The dominant fake lepton track background is estimated using a data-driven method by
measuring and applying transfer factors in dedicated CRs, as described in Section 7.2.
Separate CRs are prepared for each track pT bin shown in Table 7.1 to perform the
track pT binned transfer factor method. The SRs and VRs are prepared as track pT
inclusive regions; the track pT binned estimations are summed in these regions. All of the
procedures are implemented in the statistical fit framework, and the transfer factors and
the expected number of fake lepton background are obtained by the simultaneous fit.
117

=== PDF PAGE 129 ===
The transfer factor method is performed by first injecting dummy events into each CR
and normalizing its number to the number of events in data subtracted by the true lepton
contribution estimated by MC. Therefore, the normalization factor µCR is calculated by
µCR · ndummy = ndata ￾ ntrueLep, (9.14)
where n represents the number of events. ndummy represents the initial value used for the
fit and does not have any meanings by itself; its value multiplied by µCR represents the fake
lepton track yield in the CR. The transfer factors are then calculated by taking the ratio
of normalization factors of CR-A and CR-B as illustrated in Fig. 7.3. When the number of
dummy events are set equally for CR-A and CR-B, the ratio of the normalization factors
equals to the actual ratio of the number of events, hence the transfer factor:

10. Results
This section shows and discusses the results of the statistical analysis, which has been
described in Section 9.
10.1 Background-only fit
Fits have been performed with the background-only hypothesis to obtain the expected
number of SM background events in the SRs. The method has been first validated with
dedicated VRs as described in Section 7.4. It must be noted that the fit has been per￾formed for each of the six pNN-✓ values for both electron and muon channels. Moreover,
the relative uncertainty on the expected total background is larger for the muon chan￾nel, as the dominant non-closure uncertainty assigned to the muon channel is larger as
mentioned in Section 8.3.
Fig. 10.1 and Fig. 10.2 show the agreement between observed data and estimated back￾ground yields in the VRs for each pNN-✓, for the electron channel and the muon channel,
respectively. The deviation between the observed data and the estimated background has
been quantified by the significance Z [174] defined as

=== PDF PAGE 140 ===
10.2 Model-dependent fit
Since no significant excess has been observed in all SRs, model-dependent fits have been
performed to set exclusion limits on the target signal models, described in Sections 1.3.1
and 3.3.1. The fits have been performed for both electron and muon channels with all
six pNN-✓s: 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, and 5 GeV. Fig. 10.7 and Fig. 10.8 show the observed and
expected exclusion limits for each pNN-✓ for electron and muon channels, respectively.
All of the exclusion limits are at 95% confidence level and are shown in the m(˜￾±
1 )=
1
2
￾
m(˜￾0
2)+ m(˜￾0
1)
 vs ￾m(˜￾±
1 , ￾˜0
1) plane for easy comparison with results from other
searches.
Moreover, the six pNN-✓s have been combined to draw a single exclusion contour for
each channel. As these SRs could have possible overlaps, the envelope of the six contours
has been taken by adopting the pNN-✓ with the smallest expected CLs value for each
of the signal mass points. Fig. 10.9 shows the observed and expected exclusion limits
obtained by combining the six pNN-✓s for each channel. For the electron channel, slight
excess in data for pNN-✓ = 2 GeV and slight deficit in data for pNN-✓ = 4 GeV have
resulted in a rather distorted observed limit with respect to the expected limit. For the
muon channel, slight excess in data for pNN-✓ = 4 GeV and pNN-✓ = 5 GeV have resulted
in a looser limit for higher mass splitting values.
Up to this point, the electron and muon channels have been analyzed and evaluated
separately, however, as these channels are orthogonal to each other, they can be statis￾tically combined by performing channel inclusive fits. Channel inclusive fits have been
performed for the six pNN-✓ configurations, and the configurations have been combined
by the above method. Fig. 10.10 shows the observed and expected exclusion limits for
each pNN-✓ for the channel inclusive fits, and Fig. 10.11 shows the pNN combined ob￾served and expected exclusion limits of the channel inclusive fits. The observed limit and
the exclusion limit is similar for the channel inclusive fit due to coincidental e↵ects; the
e↵ect of excess observed in the higher mass splitting values for the muon channel has
been complemented by the e↵ect of deficit observed in similar regions for the electron
channel. The final observed exclusion limit, obtained from the statistical combination of
both electron and muon channels and the combination of six pNN-✓s, extends beyond
m(˜￾±
1 ) = 130 GeV for compressed mass regions with ￾m(˜￾±
1 , ￾˜0
1) = 1–2 GeV, which have
never been explored by experiments at the LHC.
Fig. 10.12 shows the pNN combined observed and expected exclusion limits overlaid
on results observed by existing analyses. It can be seen that the search presented in this
dissertation plays a crucial role of searching the gap between the previous soft 2L search
and the displaced track search.
129

10.3 Model-independent fit
Model-independent fits have been performed to set upper limits on the number of BSM
events in the target search regions, having an event topology with a high-pT hadronic jet,
large missing transverse momentum, a low-pT lepton, and a very-low-pT lepton-like track.
For this purpose, as summarized in Table 10.13, three dedicated “discovery” SRs have
been defined by combining the electron and muon channel SRs for three pNN-✓ values:
pNN-✓ =1, 3, and 5 GeV, corresponding to small-￾m, mid-￾m and large-￾m. Each of
them has been named as SR-small-dM, SR-mid-dM, and SR-large-dM.
Table 10.13: Summary of the discovery SRs. Three regions are defined by combining the electron and
muon channel exclusion SRs for three pNN-✓ values.
SR-small-dM SR-mid-dM SR-large-dM
pNN-✓ [GeV] 1 3 5
Fig. 10.13 shows the p-values as a function of signal strength µsig for each of the
discovery SRs, obtained from the model-independent upper limit scans. Table 10.14
summarizes the model-independent upper limits obtained from these scans. For SR￾small-dM, SR-mid-dM, and SR-large-dM, the observed upper limits on the cross section
of BSM processes are 0.05 fb, 0.13 fb, and 0.14 fb, respectively.
Table 10.14: Model-independent upper limits for SR-small-dM, SR-mid-dM, and SR-large-dM.
Observed Expected h✏￾i95
obs[fb] S95
obs S95
exp CLb p(s = 0) (Z)
SR-small-dM 7 7.97 ± 2.42 0.05 7.58.0+3.7 ￾2.2 0.41 0.50 (0.00)
SR-mid-dM 29 27.90 ± 7.42 0.13 18.6 18.1+6.5 ￾4.7 0.55 0.50 (0.01)
SR-large-dM 23 13.67 ± 3.69 0.14 20.3 12.7+5.3 ￾3.5 0.91 0.06 (1.52)

=== PDF PAGE 147 ===

(c) SR-large-dM

11. Discussion
11.1 Slight excess observed for the muon channel
Although there was no significant excesses of observed data in any of the SRs, it can be
seen in Fig. 10.4 that a slight excess of 1.5￾ has been observed in the SRs of the muon
channel for pNN-✓ = 5 GeV. It is di￾cult to determine the source of this excess without
additional statistics, however, some candidates of the source is discussed here.
Fig. 11.1 shows some of the important kinematic distributions of the observed data
and estimated background in the SR for the muon channel for pNN-✓ = 5 GeV. It can
be seen from these distributions that the excess comes from the high track pT region, in
particular, the region with pT = 3–5 GeV. Dependencies on other variables have not been
observed.
If the excess is from an actual BSM process, it is less-likely that it is from the targeted
higgsino pair production signal. This is because, the mass region which pNN-✓ = 5 GeV
targets has large overlaps with the region which has already been excluded by the soft
2L search. Moreover, the electron channel does not present a similar excess; this is also
incompatible with the higgsino signal hypothesis. Therefore, if one assumes that this
excess is from BSM, it would likely be from another process involving the high-pT jet
+ Emiss
T topology, favoring the emission of low-pT muons over low-pT electrons. On the
other hand, if the excess is from SM background, it is likely that the excess is from
the dominant W(! µ⌫)+jets background, in which a hadronic track from an underlying
event is accidentally identified as a low-pT muon track. However, hadronic tracks from
underlying events tend to have lower pT, so it is not fully clear why the excess gathers in
the high track pT regions. As mentioned earlier, more data would be necessary to further
understand the source of this excess.
11.2 Comparison to previous approaches and other
experiments
Comparison to the ATLAS soft 2L search
As shown in Fig. 10.12, the search presented in this dissertation has successfully explored
the mass splitting region with ￾m(˜￾±
1 , ￾˜0
1) = 1–1.5 GeV (or ￾m(˜￾±
1 , ￾˜0
1) = 2–3 GeV) for
the first time since the LEP2 experiment. Compared to the soft 2L search, which targets
the same event topology with high-pT hadronic jet, missing transverse momentum, and
two low-pT leptons from ˜￾0
2 ! ￾˜0
1`+`￾ decay, this search has extended the search region

=== PDF PAGE 150 ===
down to smaller mass splitting regions by the combination of the two following e↵orts:
• Significant suppression of low-pT fake lepton tracks by the development of low-pT
lepton track identification methods,
• More sophisticated signal and background separation by a pNN-based event selec￾tion method.
The soft 2L search required two standard leptons or one standard lepton plus an
unidentified isolated track. The lower pT bound for standard electrons and muons were
4.5 GeV and 3 GeV, respectively, which are the same as the standard lepton criteria
used in this search. The event selection was performed by imposing uniform cuts on all
considered mass points.
On the other hand, this search requires one standard lepton and one low-pT lepton
identified track, with lower pT bounds reaching 0.5 GeV and 1 GeV for the electron chan￾nel and muon channel, respectively. The introduction of these algorithms contributes
significantly to the suppression of low-pT fake lepton tracks, which was also the main
background of the one lepton plus one isolated track channel of the soft 2L search. Fur￾thermore, the event selection of this search is performed by a pNN, considering the event
topology dependencies on the mass splitting, as shown in Fig 6.4 and Fig. 6.5. Compared
to the dominant W+jets background, the event topology di↵er more for smaller mass
splitting values, which has also contributed to the increased sensitivity in the correspond￾ing regions.
Comparison to the CMS experiment
The CMS experiment has also performed a higgsino search similar to the ATLAS soft
2L search [175]. Fig. 11.2 shows the exclusion limits set on simplified higgsino models
set by the CMS experiment. Higgsino mass of up to 150 GeV has been excluded for
￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1) = 3 GeV, however, regions having smaller mass splittings have not been
considered. Therefore, as of today, this search sets the most stringent limit on simplified
higgsino models in the targeted mass regions of ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1) = 2–3 GeV.

11.4 Impact on SUSY mass parameters
As described in Section 1.2.3, the masses of the neutralinos are represented by the funda￾mental SUSY mass parameters µ, M1, and M2. Thus, the results shown in Section 11.3
can further be interpreted to examine the impact on these fundamental parameters.
In typical models such as NUHM models, the mass hierarchy relation are likely to
satisfy |µ| <M1 <M2. In such cases, the slight mixture of the bino component with the
dominant higgsino component could lead to a non-trivial mass splitting value between the
light mass eigenstates. Hence, in this interpretation, the impact of the wino component is
assumed to be negligible; the relation |µ| ⌧ M1 ⌧ M2 is assumed to examine the impact
of the results on only the higgsino and bino mass parameters, µ and M1. Under such
assumptions, the higgsino and bino mass parameters are given by
|µ| = m(˜￾0
1)+ 1
2
(1 + ￾ sin 2￾)￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1), (11.3)
M1 = m2
Z
s2
W
￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1)
. (11.4)
Fig. 11.4 shows the interpretation of the results of this search in the fundamental
mass parameter plane of µ vs M1, under the above assumptions. Since ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1) is
much smaller compared to m(˜￾0
1), |µ| depends only slightly on tan ￾ and ￾ according to
Eq. (11.3); they have been set to tan ￾ = 10 and ￾ = ￾1 as a benchmark scenario. It has
been observed that this search extends the limit on M1 by about 1 TeV for |µ| below 130
GeV, compared to the previous soft 2L search. As mentioned in Section 11.3, the limits
shown here do not depend on the mass of the charged higgsino ˜￾±
1 .
Figure 11.4: Interpretation of the search results in the µ vs M1 plane. |µ| ⌧ M1 ⌧ M2, tan ￾ = 10, and
￾ ⌘ sgn(µ)= ￾1 have been assumed for simplicity. This search extends the limit on M1 by about 1 TeV.

=== PDF PAGE 154 ===
11.5 Prospects in future experiments
The results presented in this dissertation uses the full LHC Run 2 dataset collected by
the ATLAS experiment, corresponding to 140 fb￾1 of proton-proton collision data at
ps = 13 TeV. It is worthwhile to estimate the expected sensitivity of this search method
in future experiments, especially with the ongoing LHC Run 3 and HL-LHC starting in
2029, to devise future search strategies. In this study, the performances of the low-pT
electron and muon track IDs in future experiments are assumed to be the same as LHC
Run 2 for simplicity.
Expected sensitivities of this search in LHC Run 3 and HL-LHC have been evaluated
as expected exclusion contours and discovery reaches by scaling the number of signal
and background yields in the SRs. The scaling factors are comprised of two elements as
summarized in Table 11.1: the integrated luminosity ratio and the cross section ratio.
The signal cross sections have been estimated to rise by about 10(15)% by the transition
from ps = 13 TeV to ps = 13.6(14) TeV. The background cross sections have been
estimated using those of the dominant W+jets processes; they are expected to increase
by 5(8)% by the energy increase in Run 3 (HL-LHC). The final significance is calculated
by the scaled signal yields, background yields, and the background uncertainties using
Eq. (10.1). Z> 1.645, corresponding to 95% CL, is required for exclusion, and Z> 5 is
required for discovery.
Table 11.1: Summary of the scaling factors used for evaluating the expected sensitivities for future
experiments. The integrated luminosity factors are derived by simply taking the ratio over 140 fb￾1.
The cross section ratios correspond to the expected increase by the increase of center-of-mass energy:
ps = 13.6 TeV for LHC Run 3 and ps = 14 TeV for HL-LHC. Note that the 400 fb￾1 of LHC Run
3 includes the Run 2 data of 140 fb￾1, thus the cross section factors have been applied only to the
corresponding 260 fb￾1.
LHC Run 3 (400 fb￾1) HL-LHC (3000 fb￾1) HL-LHC (4000 fb￾1)
Integrated luminosity 2.86 21.4 28.6
Signal cross section 1.10 1.15 1.15
Background cross section 1.05 1.08 1.08
Fig. 11.5 shows the expected exclusion and discovery reach of this search in LHC Run
3 and HL-LHC, evaluated by the above method. In the case in which the higgsino does
not exist in the corresponding search region, the exclusion limit is expected to reach up
to higgsino mass of 160 GeV and 170 GeV for LHC Run 3 and HL-LHC, respectively.
The expected discovery reaches for LHC Run 3 and HL-LHC both lie within the observed
exclusion of the search presented in this dissertation; even if higgsinos exist in this target
mass splitting region, they cannot be discovered by the ATLAS experiment with the same
strategy. This gives out a significant message that further improvements in the analysis
method are necessary to discover higgsinos in future LHC experiments.
Possible future improvements
Some improvements are proposed below for possible amelioration of the sensitivity to
higgsinos in future experiments, using the low-momentum lepton signatures targeted in
143

=== PDF PAGE 155 ===
Figure 11.5: Expected exclusion and discovery reach of this search in LHC Run 3 and HL-LHC. The
expected discovery reaches lie within the observed exclusion limit presented in this dissertation.
this search. The sensitivity of this search largely depends on the identification of the low￾momentum leptons emitted from the neutral higgsino decay, ˜￾0
2 ! ￾˜0
1`+`￾. Therefore,
the first-order improvements are expected to be of those related to the performance of
low-pT lepton identification:
• Improvement of the low-pT lepton track identification,
• Improvement of the standard lepton identification, with possible extension to lower
pT regime,
• Addition of the 0`2T channel.
The first item, the improvement of the low-pT lepton track identification, is the most
straightforward way of ameliorating the sensitivity of this search. This could be realized by
adding further information as inputs to the low-pT lepton track identification algorithms.
For example, the shape information of the topo-clusters could be added for both electron
and muon track identification, to further discriminate lepton-like tracks and hadron-like
tracks.
The second item, the improvement of the standard lepton identification is also expected
to have significant impact on the search. The current pT threshold of 4.5 (3) GeV of the
standard electron (muon) identification sets a stringent limit on this search, as signal
leptons tend to have lower pT for the target ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1) = 2–3 GeV region. However, as
the standard electron and muon identification criteria have tighter requirements on the
calorimeter energy thresholds and muon spectrometer hits, respectively, this item may
be more di￾cult to achieve, at least in the ongoing LHC Run 3 due to similar detector
performances.
The third item, the addition of the 0`2T channel, could follow after the success of the
first item. The requirement of the one standard ID lepton of this search is imposed to
144

=== PDF PAGE 156 ===
achieve su￾cient background reduction. If this could be achieved with the low-pT lepton
track identification algorithms, addition of a channel requiring no standardly identified
lepton and two low-pT lepton-like tracks, could result in further sensitivity.
In addition to the aforementioned items, improvement of the event selection method
and statistical combinations with the 2L search and the displaced track search could
also be considered. However, these improvements are expected to have only second-order
impacts on the sensitivity to higgsinos.
145

=== PDF PAGE 157 ===
12. Conclusion
The dissertation presents the search for higgsinos using 140 fb￾1 of proton-proton collisions
at center-of-mass energy of ps = 13 TeV at the LHC, collected by the ATLAS detector
in 2015–2018. The search targets final states with a high-pT hadronic jet, large Emiss
T ,
and two low-pT leptons, which is the expected event topology of higgsino pair production
events with the neutral higgsino decay ˜￾0
2 ! ￾˜0
1`+`￾. The sensitivity to higgsinos has
been drastically improved by the development and application of new low-pT electron
and muon track identification algorithms and the introduction of neural-network-based
event selection techniques which consider the kinematic dependencies on ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1). The
dominant 1L + fake lepton track background has been estimated by a data-driven method
to reduce MC modeling uncertainties on the fake lepton track. In all Signal Regions, no
significant excess of data has been observed.
Exclusion limits on the target higgsino models have been set at 95% confidence level.
In the context of the simplified higgsino model with ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1)=2￾m(˜￾±
1 , ￾˜0
1), higgsino
mass of up to 130 GeV has been excluded for target regions of ￾m(˜￾±
1 , ￾˜0
1) = 1–2 GeV.
The limits in terms of higgsino mass have been extended by 40 GeV, compared to the
results from the LEP2 experiment. This is the first search at the LHC to probe such mass
splitting regions; this search has successfully contributed to covering the “higgsino gap”
with the LHC.
146

=== PDF PAGE 158 ===
13. Contributions to the Level-1 Muon
Trigger System in Run 3
The muon trigger system is crucial for probing new physics in the ATLAS experiment;
although the search presented in this dissertation does not directly use the muon trigger, it
is exploited in many of the e￾ciency measurements of the objects used in this search, such
as the muon and the low-pT muon track in the muon channel. In addition to the search for
higgsinos, significant e↵orts have been put into leading the development, commissioning,
and the operation of the TGC detector and Level-1 muon trigger systems for LHC Run
3. This section briefly summarizes the contributions, primarily made during my Master’s
program in 2019–2021.
13.1 Run 3 upgrade of the Level-1 muon trigger
As described earlier in Section 2.2.7, the ATLAS Level-1 muon trigger is a hardware￾based system, which triggers physics events of interest by identifying muon candidates in
the RPC and TGC detectors for barrel and endcap regions, respectively. For LHC Run
3, the Level-1 endcap muon trigger has been significantly upgraded from Run 2; New
Small Wheel (NSW) [177] detector and other inner muon detectors have been introduced
to reduce fake muon trigger signals originating from protons emerging from the beam
pipe and toroid magnets, as illustrated in Fig. 13.1. By taking the coincidence between
the TGC and the inner muon detectors, reduction of fake muon trigger signals has been
achieved as shown in Fig. 13.2 [178].
Since the inputs to the trigger logic have largely increased due to the introduction
of new detectors, the backend trigger logic boards had to be newly designed. Therefore,
New Sector Logic (New SL) boards [179], capable of processing the endcap muon trigger
using the inputs from TGC and NSW detectors, have been developed. The surrounding
systems, which for example handle clock signal distribution, trigger readout, and trigger
output processing, have also been developed accordingly.
13.2 Realization of integrated control
In the summer of 2019, the development of the Run 3 Level-1 muon trigger was still at its
preparatory stage, where tests of the new electronics, including the New SL boards, were
still carried out at single board level. The implementation of the new electronics and their
corresponding software applications to the ATLAS control system was of utmost priority
147

=== PDF PAGE 159 ===
Figure 13.1: A schematic illustration of detectors involved in the Run 3 Level-1 endcap muon trigger and
the fake muon trigger signals.

=== PDF PAGE 160 ===
to proceed with further commissioning of the system.
This has been achieved by redesigning and developing the TDAQ software and database
necessary to systematically control the whole system. The Run 3 Level-1 muon trigger
system has been successfully synchronized to the legacy Run 2 TGC frontend system by
scrutinization of the initialization procedures of each electronic device and the communica￾tion procedures of each optical link between the electronics. Furthermore, the robustness
of the system has been enhanced by the implementation of monitoring functionalities of
the new electronics, which are capable of detecting possible erros and hardware failures.
The softwares and functions described here are currently in use in the ongoing Run 3
data-taking.
13.3 Development of a new software-based readout
system
For o✏ine validation and performance evaluation of the Run 3 Level-1 endcap muon
trigger algorithm, the trigger logic input and output data must be read out. This was
originally done by a hardware-based system in Run 2, however, a new software-based
readout system called Software-based Readout Driver (SROD) [180], has been developed
for Run 3 to improve flexibility and to cope with large increase in trigger input data.
Stable trigger readout has been achieved at 100 kHz by scrutinization and modification
of the event-building software and evaluating its performance.
Fig. 13.3 shows an overview of the SROD and its software structure. The SROD is
designed to run on a commercially available high-performance servers. A single SROD
receives trigger inputs and outputs from 12 New SL boards via TCP/IP protocol. It
adopts a multi-process architecture to collect data and build the event data in parallel.
The “EventBuilder” application is responsible for formatting the final event data, which is
sent out the ATLAS Readout System (ROS). Therefore, the EventBuilder application has
the heaviest load, and its performance determines the performance of the SROD system.
For stable operation at the nominal Level-1 trigger rate of 100 kHz, the event-building
must be processed within 10 µs on average.
The processing speed of the EventBuilder application has been improved by countless
code optimizations including the reduction of certain variable types and the use of array
of function pointers. As a result, the performance of the SROD reached well beyond that
was expected to be required for Run 3 as shown in Fig. 13.4. The performances shown
in these figures have been evaluated in 2021, however, further modifications have been
carried out before the start of Run 3 in 2022.
13.4 Commissioning and stable operation
Significant contributions have been made not only by the developments of the softwares
and systems, but also by means of commissioning and stable operation. Contributions
to the commissioning include the one with ATLAS central trigger system immediately
before Run 3; the timing of the endcap muon trigger signals, crucial for data-taking,
has been successfully adjusted by analyzing signals from cosmic muon runs and initial
149

Figure 13.3: Overview of the SROD system used in the ATLAS Level-1 endcap muon trigger in Run 3
[180]. The software structure of the SROD is shown in detail.
(a) EventBuilder processing speed as a function of data
size
(b) EventBuilder processing speed as a function of trig￾ger rate
Figure 13.4: The performance of EventBuilder application in the SROD, as a function of data size and
trigger rate [180]. At the nominal trigger rate of 100 kHz, the SROD system can handle up to 4800 bits
per event, which is more than 2.5 times larger than the expected size of 1750 bits in Run 3. For a fixed
data size of 1760 bits per event, the SROD system can run stably up to the speed of 170 kHz, which is
well beyond the nominal trigger rate.
150

=== PDF PAGE 162 ===
proton-proton collision runs. Contributions to the stable operation of the system include,
• Handling unexpected errors and promptly recovering the system during physics
data-taking,
• Supporting team members to deal with such problems on-site at CERN and remotely
from Tokyo,
which have been done countless times in 2022–2023.
13.5 Summary
Through the developments and implementations of the new Level-1 muon trigger system,
its tests with surrounding systems and adjustment of signals have been realized in an op￾portune manner. Consequently, the Level-1 muon trigger system has successfully started
its operation at the start of LHC Run 3, realizing stable data-taking necessary for further
physics analysis.

B. Details of Low-pT Lepton Track Iden￾tification
B.1 Input variables of LPET ID DNN
This section summarizes the data/MC distributions of the inputs used for the LPET ID
DNN. Since it is di￾cult to extract only real electron tracks in data, the distributions
for all tracks are presented for reference. In particular, for events passing the event
preselection criteria in Table 6.1, distributions for all tracks passing the selection criteria in
Table 6.2 with flipped charge requirement (same-sign, SS) and higher track pT requirement
of pT > 1 GeV are shown. The higher track pT requirement is imposed to briefly reduce
background tracks. For MC, all background samples listed in Table 3.3 have been used.
Figures B.1–B.4, Figure B.5 and Figure B.6 show the distributions of track, TRT,
and calo inputs used for the LPET ID DNN, respectively. There are some variables with
poor data/MC modelling (e.g. eProbabilityComb and TRTHighThresholdHits), which
are thought to be the main cause of the small scale factors of LPET ID WP, shown
in Figure 5.19. However, these variables have high importance in the identification of
electron tracks, hence have been kept as LPET ID inputs.

B.2 Performance measurement and calibration
This section describes the samples and event selection criteria used for the performance
measurement and calibration of LPET ID. Target events are Z ! ee￾ processes as de￾scribed in Section 5.1.3.
B.2.1 MC samples
Table B.1 summarizes the MC samples used for the performance measurement. All sam￾ples have been simulated with GEANT4 [141], and simulation of the e↵ects of pileup is
performed as described in Section 3.3.3.
Table B.1: List of background MC samples used for the performance measurement of LPET ID.
Physics process Generator Parton shower Tune
Z ! ee￾ Sherpa 2.2.11 Sherpa Sherpa
Z ! ee Sherpa 2.2.11 Sherpa Sherpa
Drell-Yan ee Powheg-box v1 Pythia v8.186 AZNLO CTEQ6L1
B.2.2 Event and track selection criteria
The performance measurement of LPET ID targets Z ! ee￾ processes as described in
Section 5.1.3. Table B.2 shows the event selection criteria. Selections have been optimized
to suppress Z ! ee background events as much as possible, without discarding too much
signal Z ! ee￾ events.
Each of the selected events by the above criteria usually have multiple track candi￾dates surrounding the photon, most of them being background hadronic or fake tracks.
These background tracks must be reduced as much as possible to estimate the number
of signal electron tracks coming from Z ! ee￾ processes. Table B.3 summarizes the
track preselection criteria for reducing the background tracks. The selection criteria have
been optimized to select signal electron tracks and suppress other background tracks. All
tracks passing these criteria are used in the performance measurement of LPET ID.
B.2.3 dR(trk, ￾) distributions for each track pT bin
Figures in this section show the dR(trk, ￾) distributions used in the LPET ID performance
measurement for each track pT bin. Figure B.7 shows the MC shape distributions used
for the fits. Figure B.8 shows the post-fit distributions for WP pass tracks. Figure B.9
shows the post-fit distributions for WP fail tracks.
B.2.4 E￾ciency and scale factors for fake electron tracks
Although the e￾ciency and scale factors for fake electron tracks are not used in the
analysis, it is worthwhile to mention them here. E￾ciency for fake electron tracks could
also be obtained as done for true electron tracks, by simply counting the number of
fake electron tracks passing and failing the LPET ID WP from the post-fit dR(trk, ￾)

distribution. Figure B.10 shows the LPET ID e￾ciency for true and fake electron tracks
for both Z ! ee￾ data and MC. The e￾ciency for fake electron tracks have a relation
between data and MC similar to the true electron tracks; the e￾ciency for data is lower
compared to MC. Figure B.11 shows the scale factors for true and electron tracks, obtained
by taking the ratios of Figure B.10a and Figure B.10b. The scale factors for fake electron
tracks are comparable to those of true electron tracks for pT < 2 GeV, whereas they are
relatively smaller for pT > 2 GeV. This indicates that the signal to background ratio of
LPET ID is rather higher for data compared to what has been evaluated using MC.

D. Signal contamination
The signal contamination for each region used in the analysis has been checked to verify
the validity of the background estimation method.
D.1 Control Regions
Figures D.1-D.3 and D.4-D.6 show the signal contamination in CR-A, CR-B, and CR-C
used for estimating the background in the SRs for the electron channel and the muon chan￾nel, respectively. For both channels, contamination in CR-A is the most significant, espe￾cially for mass points with large ￾m(˜￾0
2, ￾˜0
1) for small pNN-✓ values. The contaminations
are around 15% and 10% at maximum for the electron and muon channel, respectively
and are su￾ciently small. Moreover, the corresponding mass points with relatively large
mass splittings have already been excluded by previous analyses, therefore, the e↵ects of
signal contamination for these mass points have minimal impact on the analysis.
D.2 Validation Regions
Figures D.7–D.9 and D.10–D.12 show the signal contamination in VRs for the electron
channel and muon channel, respectively. Out of the three VRs, VR-highLarge has the
most signal contamination for both channels, however, the values remain su￾ciently small,
typically below 10%.
